# Kimi decode optimization notes

Scope: only batch=1 decode speed matters, i.e. the steady `--benchmark` token/s.

Model:

```sh
/raid/gguf/kimi/Kimi-K2.6-UD-Q4_K_XL-00001-of-00014.gguf
```

## Current Code State

- Added raw Q4_0 expert weight retention in `tinygrad/llm/gguf.py` when `Q4_EXPERT`, `CUSTOM_KIMI_MOE`, or `CUSTOM_KIMI_MOE_Q8` is enabled.
- Added `tinygrad/llm/amd_kimi.py` custom AMD kernels for Kimi expert FFN:
  - selected Q4_0 gate/up
  - selected Q4_0 down/reduce
  - Q8_0 activation quantization
  - Q4_0 x Q8_0 gate/up
  - Q4_0 x Q8_0 down/reduce
  - direct `gate/up -> Q8_0` path to avoid materializing fp32/fp16 gate_up before down
- Fixed raw Q4 expert pointer offsets to use `size_t`; this matters for 384 experts because raw expert tensors exceed 2GB.
- `tinygrad/llm/model.py` now routes `CUSTOM_KIMI_MOE_Q8=1` through:

```py
kimi_gate_up_q4_q8_to_q8_0(...)
kimi_down_reduce_q4_q8_from_q8_0(...)
```

- Added `CUSTOM_KIMI_ATTN_QAKV_HALF=1` for Kimi MLA blocks. It precomputes a per-block half `attn_q_a + attn_kv_a_mqa` concatenated weight after load and replaces two `7168 -> 1536/576` projections with one `7168 -> 2112` projection, then slices the result back into q and kv paths.
- Retuned `kimi_down_reduce_q4_q8_0` to defer the cross-warp selected-down reduce until all topk partials are written, preserving the original final accumulation order while removing per-topk synchronizations.

## Validated Results

Baseline layer-shard 4 steady decode was about 33 tok/s.

Current best command:

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 JITBEAM=8 REALIZE_SMALL=8000000 \
CUSTOM_KIMI_MOE_Q8=1 CUSTOM_KIMI_SHARED_Q8=1 CUSTOM_KIMI_ROUTER_TOPK=1 CUSTOM_KIMI_OUTPUT_ARGMAX_Q8=1 \
CUSTOM_KIMI_ATTN_QAKV_HALF=1 \
PYTHONPATH=. \
python3 tinygrad/llm/cli.py \
  --model /raid/gguf/kimi/Kimi-K2.6-UD-Q4_K_XL-00001-of-00014.gguf \
  --shard 3 --benchmark 12
```


Generic kernel mapping from VIZ UOps:

```text
r_64_96_8_8_2_6_4       attn_q_b, 1536 -> 12288 Q8_0 GEMV
r_896_8_8_8_8_4_4       attn_output, 8192 -> 7168 Q8_0 GEMV
r_768_224_2_32          attn_q_a, 7168 -> 1536 Q8_0 GEMV
r_6_32_224_3_32         attn_kv_a_mqa, 7168 -> 576 Q8_0 GEMV
r_512_224_4_32          shared expert gate, 7168 -> 2048 Q8_0 GEMV + silu
r_512_32_7_4_32         shared expert up, 7168 -> 2048 Q8_0 GEMV + multiply
r_1792_64_4_32          shared expert down, 2048 -> 7168 Q8_0 GEMV + add/residual
r_384_64_4_7_4          router, 7168 -> 384 fp16/Q8 path + sigmoid
r_36_64_8_4_2_32        MLA q/k preparation and cache/rope related work
r_128_8_16_8_16_2       MLA v_b, 512 -> 8192 Q8_0 GEMV
```

Interpretation:

- The custom expert MoE path is now only about 24-25% of profiled graph time.
- The remaining larger bucket is tinygrad generic Q8_0 GEMV/dequant work, especially attention projections and shared expert projections.
- Further optimizing only selected Q4 expert kernels has limited upside now.
- The next promising direction is Kimi-specific Q8_0 GEMV/fused Q8_0 paths, but only if microbench shows real gain including activation quantization cost.

## GPU Incident

Do not rerun GPU work until the machine/GPU is recovered.

Failed command:

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 JITBEAM=2 PYTHONPATH=. python3 /tmp/b1/bench_q8_gemv_custom.py
```

Problem:

- `/tmp/b1/bench_q8_gemv_custom.py` initially missed `if __name__ == "__main__"`.
- `JITBEAM=2` used Python multiprocessing spawn.
- Spawn children re-imported the script and executed the benchmark again.
- This created many Python/spawn child processes and then AMD reported:

```text
HW fault: reset_type=0 reset_cause=0 memory_lost=1 gpu_id=63738
```

The script has since been patched with a main guard, but it has not been safely rerun.

Before any future GPU run:

```sh
pgrep -af 'python.*(tinygrad|cli.py|bench_|profile_|llm)|python3 -|resource_tracker' || true
```

If residual processes remain, ask before killing them.

## AITER/vLLM Source Notes

There is no usable AITER ROCm runtime environment on this machine, so AITER can only be used as source reference here. Do not treat any local AITER microbench as validated.

Source evidence:

- vLLM's Kimi-K2.5 AMD recipe uses `VLLM_ROCM_USE_AITER=1`, `VLLM_ROCM_QUICK_REDUCE_QUANTIZATION=INT4`, `--tensor-parallel-size 8`, and `--block-size=1`.
- vLLM AITER MoE routes through `rocm_aiter_ops.fused_moe(...)`, not a generic PyTorch/tinygrad dequant+matmul path.
- AITER `fused_moe.py` has decode-specific logic:
  - `get_ksplit(...)` only enables split-K when `token * topk <= expert`, i.e. the batch=1/topk=8 Kimi shape is explicitly targeted.
  - `use_nt(...)` enables non-temporal load when `token * topk // expert < 64`, also true for batch=1.
  - tuned config lookup chooses `block_m`, `ksplit`, and exact kernel names.
- AITER Kimi FP4 tuned config has token=1 rows for `model_dim=7168`, `expert=384`, `topk=8`, `inter_dim=256/512`, around 22-25 us total on its recorded target. This is not directly comparable to our GGUF Q4_0 path, but it shows AITER has a special fixed-shape Kimi decode kernel family.
- vLLM QuickReduce supports ROCm all-reduce quantization regimes including INT4, but it is tied to vLLM's TP path and supported world sizes. This is separate from current tinygrad layer shard.

Interpretation:

- The reported vLLM/AITER speed should not be read as proof that tinygrad layer shard can scale. vLLM is using a different stack: AITER fused MoE, fixed-shape/tuned kernels, TP=8, block-size=1, and INT4 quick reduce.
- The source does support one actionable idea for tinygrad: reproduce AITER's single-GPU decode tricks first, especially selected/tuned MoE and split-K occupancy, before trying cross-GPU TP again.

## Q4 Expert Preshuffle Microbench

Script:

```sh
/tmp/b1/bench_q4_preshuffle.py
```

This tests a load-time preshuffle candidate for selected expert gate/up:

- current layout: separate `gate_w` and `up_w` Q4_0 raw buffers
- preshuffled layout: one interleaved `gate|up` Q4_0 buffer, so each selected expert row/block reads gate and up from adjacent memory

The benchmark pre-quantizes input to Q8_0 once and times only the selected Q4 x Q8 gate/up -> Q8 kernel. It does not include one-time load-time conversion cost.

8 expert slice:

```text
raw separate       kernel median 52.88 us, min 50.84 us
gate_up interlv    kernel median 46.16 us, min 44.08 us
```

384 expert address space with spread selection `sel=[0, 54, 108, 162, 216, 270, 324, 383]`:

```text
raw separate       kernel median 52.52 us, min 50.92 us
gate_up interlv    kernel median 46.28 us, min 44.64 us
```

Interpretation:

- Interleaving gate/up Q4 expert weights has a repeatable kernel-level gain of about 12-13% for the gate/up custom kernel.
- This is real but small in full decode terms. Current profile had gate/up custom at about 4.38 ms per steady graph, so this likely saves roughly 0.5-0.6 ms/token if integrated cleanly.
- Expected full decode impact is around 2%, not a path to 70 tok/s by itself.
- It is still a reasonable low-risk optimization because it is load-time layout only and does not introduce multi-GPU communication.

Integration attempt:

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 JITBEAM=2 CUSTOM_KIMI_MOE_Q8=1 CUSTOM_KIMI_SHARED_Q8=1 CUSTOM_KIMI_GATEUP_INTERLEAVE=1 PYTHONPATH=. \
python3 tinygrad/llm/cli.py \
  --model /raid/gguf/kimi/Kimi-K2.6-UD-Q4_K_XL-00001-of-00014.gguf \
  --shard 4 --benchmark 6
```

Two production wiring attempts failed:

```text
dummy replacement for gate/up weight: tried to allocate a 21.00 GB dummy parameter during JIT
keep original raw + attach interleaved raw: OOM while allocating a 5.91 GB interleaved buffer, used 188.21 GB on AMD
```

Conclusion: full-model preshuffle is not viable with the current loader/memory layout. It would need a loader that writes interleaved raw directly without ever keeping separate gate/up raw, which is too intrusive for an expected ~0.5 ms/token gain.

GGUF tensor-order check on the 14 split files:

```text
split.count = 14
block_count = 61
typ = 2 for ffn_{down,gate,up}_exps.weight
expert tensor order within most layers: down, gate, ..., up
split boundaries often separate gate and up, for example blk.5 gate is in split 00002 and blk.5 up is first in split 00003
```

Implication: a no-peak-memory preshuffle cannot be a local `_ggml_tensor` change. It would need `gguf_load` to keep pending gate buffers across tensor entries and sometimes across split files, allocate the final interleaved buffer first, copy gate/up directly into their interleaved positions, and avoid constructing normal raw/dequant gate/up tensors that keep the original raw buffers alive. This is possible but significantly more invasive than a kernel-only change.

Limited production attempt:

- Added a temporary `CUSTOM_KIMI_GATEUP_INTERLEAVE=1` implementation that only interleaved gate/up pairs appearing in the same GGUF split.
- Cross-split layers fell back to the original separate gate/up raw path.
- The loader used broadcast placeholders for gate/up weights and kept the interleaved raw on the placeholder attribute.

Real Kimi command:

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 JITBEAM=2 \
CUSTOM_KIMI_MOE_Q8=1 CUSTOM_KIMI_SHARED_Q8=1 CUSTOM_KIMI_ROUTER_TOPK=1 CUSTOM_KIMI_OUTPUT_ARGMAX_Q8=1 CUSTOM_KIMI_GATEUP_INTERLEAVE=1 \
PYTHONPATH=. python3 tinygrad/llm/cli.py \
  --model /raid/gguf/kimi/Kimi-K2.6-UD-Q4_K_XL-00001-of-00014.gguf \
  --shard 3 --benchmark 8
```

Observed:

```text
using model "Kimi-K2.6" with 279,298,083,200 bytes and 1,026,408,232,448 params
52375.94 ms,   0.02 tok/s,    0.35 GB/s, 18372/584279 MB  --  [BOS] the
6683.63 ms,   0.15 tok/s,   48.15 GB/s, 321842/584279 MB  --  [BOS] the the
5158.45 ms,   0.19 tok/s,   62.41 GB/s, 321924/584287 MB  --  [BOS] the the the
21.55 ms, 46.40 tok/s
21.47 ms, 46.57 tok/s
21.45 ms, 46.62 tok/s
21.50 ms, 46.50 tok/s
21.44 ms, 46.64 tok/s
```

Conclusion: reverted the production attempt. It loaded without immediate OOM, but correctness failed (`[BOS] the the ...` instead of the known `emppen...` sequence), memory accounting jumped to about 322GB, and speed did not beat the `--shard 3` baseline at up to 46.8 tok/s. The likely bug is placeholder/raw attribute propagation through `load_state_dict`, but even a fixed version would need to address the large realized interleaved raw footprint before this can be a safe route.

## Fixed-shape Q8_0 GEMV/Fusion Microbench

Script:

```sh
/tmp/b1/bench_q8_fixed_gemv.py
```

Shape tested first:

```text
shared expert gate/up: K=7168, N=2048
```

This compares current tinygrad Q8_0 lazy dequant/matmul expression against a fixed-shape custom kernel:

- generic path: two Q8_0 lazy dequant GEMV kernels, then `silu(gate) * up`
- custom path: quantize activation to Q8_0, then one fused Q8_0 x Q8_0 gate/up kernel that computes `silu(gate) * up`

Wall-time from the Python loop is polluted by schedule/expression construction, so the decision should use PROFILE GPU timestamps.

PROFILE GPU kernel times:

```text
generic gate GEMV     median 29.04 us
generic up GEMV       median 27.96 us
generic total         about 57.00 us

custom fused gate/up  median 17.28 us
activation q8         median 11.04 us
custom e2e total      about 28.32 us
```

Interpretation:

- This is a much stronger signal than Q4 preshuffle.
- Even charging a fresh activation quantization, fixed-shape fused Q8_0 shared-expert gate/up is about 2x faster at the GPU kernel level for this shape.
- In the real model, activation Q8 can potentially be shared with the selected expert custom Q4/Q8 gate/up in the same MoE block, which would make the custom gate/up path closer to 17 us than 28 us.
- Expected full decode impact is plausibly around 1.3-2.0 ms/token for 60 layers if integrated cleanly, depending on whether activation quantization is reused.
- This is now a higher-priority integration target than Q4 expert preshuffle.

## Shared Expert Q8_0 Integration

Implemented behind:

```sh
CUSTOM_KIMI_SHARED_Q8=1
```

Code changes:

- `tinygrad/llm/gguf.py`: preserve raw Q8_0 blocks for `*_shexp.weight` when `CUSTOM_KIMI_SHARED_Q8=1`.
- `tinygrad/llm/amd_kimi.py`:
  - added `kimi_gate_up_q4_q8_to_q8_from_q8_0(...)` so selected expert gate/up can reuse an already-quantized activation
  - added `kimi_shared_gate_up_q8_from_q8_0(...)` for fixed-shape shared expert gate/up, `7168 -> 2048`
- `tinygrad/llm/model.py`: when `CUSTOM_KIMI_MOE_Q8=1` and `CUSTOM_KIMI_SHARED_Q8=1`, quantize `h` to Q8_0 once, reuse it for selected MoE gate/up and shared expert gate/up, then keep shared down on the existing path.

Validated command:

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 JITBEAM=2 CUSTOM_KIMI_MOE_Q8=1 CUSTOM_KIMI_SHARED_Q8=1 PYTHONPATH=. \
python3 tinygrad/llm/cli.py \
  --model /raid/gguf/kimi/Kimi-K2.6-UD-Q4_K_XL-00001-of-00014.gguf \
  --shard 4 --benchmark 8
```

Observed steady output:

```text
23.44 ms, 42.66 tok/s
23.30 ms, 42.91 tok/s
23.36 ms, 42.80 tok/s
23.34 ms, 42.85 tok/s
23.30 ms, 42.91 tok/s
```

Compared with the previous best `CUSTOM_KIMI_MOE_Q8=1` path at about 40 tok/s, this is about a 7% decode improvement.

Rechecked after reverting shared-down experiments and preserving raw Q8_0 only for shared gate/up:

```text

23.36 ms, 42.81 tok/s, 23900/584290 MB
```

## Shared Expert Down Microbench

Tested shared expert down `2048 -> 7168` after the shared gate/up win.

Standalone Q8 activation + Q8_0 weight down looked faster at the kernel level:

```text
bench_q8_down_2048_7168 median 15.96 us
generic down supporting kernels around 11-12 us each
```

But the useful end-to-end variants did not beat the kept path:

```text
quant + q8 down: extra activation quantization erased most of the win
gate/up directly to q8 + q8 down: gate/up->q8 median 35.04 us, worse than gate/up->f32
f32 activation x q8 down: isolated kernel median 21.24 us, but real Kimi regressed
```

Real model check with the f32 custom down integrated:

```text
23.84 ms, 41.94 tok/s
23.62 ms, 42.33 tok/s
23.68 ms, 42.23 tok/s
23.63 ms, 42.32 tok/s
23.65 ms, 42.29 tok/s
```

This is slower than the gate/up-only shared expert path at about 42.8-42.9 tok/s, so the down integration was reverted. The production path should keep only shared gate/up Q8 and leave shared down on the existing generic path.

## 42.8 tok/s Profile Budget

Command:

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 PROFILE=1 NO_COLOR=1 JITBEAM=2 CUSTOM_KIMI_MOE_Q8=1 CUSTOM_KIMI_SHARED_Q8=1 PYTHONPATH=. \
python3 tinygrad/llm/cli.py \
  --model /raid/gguf/kimi/Kimi-K2.6-UD-Q4_K_XL-00001-of-00014.gguf \
  --shard 4 --benchmark 6
```

`PROFILE=1` slows the steady token from about 23.3 ms to about 33.7 ms, so only use the kernel mix, not the wall time.

Stable decode raw-kernel budget, excluding graph wrapper and `item` wait:

```text
tinygrad generic kernels       about 22.8 ms/token
selected expert gate/up        about  4.3 ms/token
selected expert down           about  3.6 ms/token
shared expert gate/up          about  1.4 ms/token
MoE input Q8 quant             about  0.6 ms/token
copy                           about  0.1 ms/token
```

Interpretation:

- The selected expert custom path is still important, but it is no longer the majority of decode time.
- The next big bucket is generic tinygrad kernels, so attention/shared/dense fixed-shape paths must beat already beam-optimized kernels including activation quant cost.
- Any standalone microbench must be run with `JITBEAM=2`; without it, generic GEMV looks much slower and gives false positives.

## Fixed-Shape Q8 Attention Microbench

Script:

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 PROFILE=1 NO_COLOR=1 JITBEAM=2 PYTHONPATH=. ITERS=128 \
python3 /tmp/b1/bench_q8_attention.py
```

Tested raw Q8_0 weight x pre-quantized Q8 activation GEMV against current generic fp16 GEMV for attention shapes:

```text
attn_q_a       7168 -> 1536   generic 17.6 us   custom 15.3 us
attn_kv_a_mqa  7168 -> 576    generic 15.0 us   custom 14.9 us
attn_q_b       1536 -> 12288  generic 19.7 us   custom 18.2 us
attn_output    8192 -> 7168   generic 36.7 us   custom 30.4 us
```

This excludes activation quantization. Once quantization is charged, the first three are not profitable and `attn_output` is at best marginal. No model integration for this route.

## Router TopK Fusion

Kimi router config:

```text
expert_count=384
expert_used_count=8
expert_weights_norm=True
expert_weights_scale=2.8269999027252197
```

Microbench script:

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 PROFILE=1 NO_COLOR=1 JITBEAM=2 PYTHONPATH=. ITERS=2048 \
python3 /tmp/b1/bench_router_topk.py
```

The first custom kernel selected topk in one thread and was too slow. The second version uses parallel reduction and matches `pairwise_topk` exactly when reading the Kimi bias as fp16.

Kernel-level profile:

```text
generic router topk graph   about 50 us
custom router topk graph    about 40 us
```

Integrated behind:

```sh
CUSTOM_KIMI_ROUTER_TOPK=1
```

Correctness check:

```text
custom sel/probs matched tensor ops on random logits with fp16 bias
real Kimi output sequence stayed `[BOS] | `emppen`='001`
```

Real Kimi 4-GPU decode:

```text
run 1:
23.27 ms, 42.98 tok/s
23.14 ms, 43.21 tok/s
23.22 ms, 43.07 tok/s
23.28 ms, 42.95 tok/s

run 2:
23.14 ms, 43.21 tok/s
23.18 ms, 43.15 tok/s
23.14 ms, 43.22 tok/s
23.17 ms, 43.17 tok/s
```

Compared with the previous 42.8-42.9 tok/s path, this is a small but repeatable improvement.

## Local 2-GPU TP GEMV Microbench

Script:

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 PROFILE=1 NO_COLOR=1 JITBEAM=2 PYTHONPATH=. ITERS=256 \
python3 /tmp/b1/bench_tp_gemv.py
```

Shapes:

```text
attn_q_b      1536 -> 12288
attn_output   8192 -> 7168
```

Results:

```text
attn_q_b single       268.52 us
attn_q_b tp_best      493.31 us   # x pre-replicated, no output gather
attn_q_b tp_gather    502.79 us

attn_output single    224.27 us
attn_output tp_best   484.41 us
attn_output tp_gather 504.25 us
```

Conclusion: row-splitting an isolated GEMV across 2 GPUs is not promising for batch=1 in this stack. Even the optimistic case with pre-replicated activation is slower than single GPU, so partial TP should not be attempted for these attention GEMVs.

## Fused Attention Q_A + KV_A Q8 Microbench

Tested a more specific attention path than the earlier independent Q8 GEMVs:

```text
attn_q_a       7168 -> 1536
attn_kv_a_mqa  7168 -> 576
```

Microbench script:

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 PROFILE=1 NO_COLOR=1 JITBEAM=2 PYTHONPATH=. ITERS=256 \
python3 /tmp/b1/bench_q8_qakv_fused.py
```

GPU profile signal:

```text
generic q_a + kv_a kernels  about 31.6 us/layer
custom quant + q8 fused     about 28.4 us/layer
```

This looked barely positive in isolation, so it was wired behind `CUSTOM_KIMI_ATTN_QAKV_Q8=1` and tested in the real model.

Real Kimi `--shard 4`:

```text
run 1:
23.22 ms, 43.07 tok/s
23.08 ms, 43.33 tok/s

run 2:
23.23 ms, 43.05 tok/s

```

The output sequence stayed unchanged, but this did not beat the router-only path. The model integration was removed; no commit for this route.

## Selected Expert Down 64-Thread Kernel

After the router win, the profiled steady decode still had selected expert down around 3.6 ms/token. The original Q4 x Q8 down kernel used one warp per output row. A fixed-shape microbench tested 32, 64, and 128 threads per row:

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 PROFILE=1 NO_COLOR=1 JITBEAM=2 PYTHONPATH=. ITERS=128 \
python3 /tmp/b1/bench_down_threads.py
```

Result with 384 experts and spread selection:

```text
current32     1495.93 us
t64           1415.57 us
t128          1570.20 us
```

The 64-thread variant splits the 64 Q4 blocks of each down row across two warps and then reduces the two warp partials through shared memory. Simply changing `THREADS` without the shared reduction would be incorrect.

Integrated by changing only `kimi_down_reduce_q4_q8_0` to 64 threads/row. Real Kimi command:

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 JITBEAM=2 CUSTOM_KIMI_MOE_Q8=1 CUSTOM_KIMI_SHARED_Q8=1 CUSTOM_KIMI_ROUTER_TOPK=1 PYTHONPATH=. \
python3 tinygrad/llm/cli.py \
  --model /raid/gguf/kimi/Kimi-K2.6-UD-Q4_K_XL-00001-of-00014.gguf \
  --shard 4 --benchmark 8
```

Two real runs:

```text
run 1:
22.71 ms, 44.03 tok/s
22.47 ms, 44.51 tok/s
22.46 ms, 44.52 tok/s
22.56 ms, 44.33 tok/s
22.54 ms, 44.37 tok/s

run 2:
22.57 ms, 44.30 tok/s
22.42 ms, 44.59 tok/s
22.40 ms, 44.64 tok/s
22.43 ms, 44.59 tok/s
22.45 ms, 44.54 tok/s
```

Compared with the previous router-only best around 43.2 tok/s, this is about a 3% decode improvement.

Validation:

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 python3 -m ruff check tinygrad/llm/amd_kimi.py
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 python3 -m unittest test.unit.test_llm_moe
```

## Selected Expert Gate/Up XQ Staging

The selected `gate/up -> Q8_0` kernel uses one CTA for 32 output rows so it can quantize those 32 rows into one Q8_0 block. That makes simple 2-warps-per-row splitting unattractive because it would either require 2048 threads/CTA or a second quantization pass.

Instead, the CTA now stages the shared input activation Q8_0 block into shared memory once. Before this, each of the 32 row warps reread the same 7168-wide `xq` activation stream.

Microbench:

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 PROFILE=1 NO_COLOR=1 JITBEAM=2 PYTHONPATH=. ITERS=128 \
python3 /tmp/b1/bench_gateup_xq_staging.py
```

Result:

```text
current        928.01 us
staged         908.83 us
```

Real Kimi command was the same current-best command:

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 JITBEAM=2 CUSTOM_KIMI_MOE_Q8=1 CUSTOM_KIMI_SHARED_Q8=1 CUSTOM_KIMI_ROUTER_TOPK=1 PYTHONPATH=. \
python3 tinygrad/llm/cli.py \
  --model /raid/gguf/kimi/Kimi-K2.6-UD-Q4_K_XL-00001-of-00014.gguf \
  --shard 4 --benchmark 8
```

Two real runs:

```text
run 1:
22.24 ms, 44.96 tok/s
22.07 ms, 45.32 tok/s
22.12 ms, 45.21 tok/s
22.16 ms, 45.12 tok/s
22.07 ms, 45.31 tok/s

run 2:
22.33 ms, 44.77 tok/s
22.17 ms, 45.10 tok/s
22.07 ms, 45.31 tok/s
22.10 ms, 45.26 tok/s
22.11 ms, 45.24 tok/s
```

Compared with the 64-thread down kernel path around 44.3-44.6 tok/s, this is another repeatable decode improvement.

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 PROFILE=1 NO_COLOR=1 JITBEAM=2 PYTHONPATH=. ITERS=256 \
```

### Gate/Up Direct Q4 Bitpack

After `xpack_w32`, the kernel still decomposed the four Q4 bytes into scalar bytes and repacked `(nibble - 8)` with `pack4_i8`. Tested direct bit operations that convert four packed Q4 nibbles into four signed int8 lanes:

```text
q4lo(p) = sign_extend_4bit_lanes((p & 0x0f0f0f0f) ^ 0x08080808)
q4hi(p) = sign_extend_4bit_lanes(((p >> 4) & 0x0f0f0f0f) ^ 0x08080808)
```

First checked the mapping against the scalar `(nibble - 8)` formula for all two-byte combinations on CPU.

Microbench:

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 PROFILE=1 NO_COLOR=1 JITBEAM=2 PYTHONPATH=. ITERS=512 \
python3 /tmp/b1/bench_gateup_xpack.py
```

Wall-time:

```text

xpack          900.22 us
xpack_w32      903.05 us
bitpack        902.58 us
```

PROFILE kernel view:

```text

xpack_w32                              42.74 us
bitpack                                36.47 us
```

Integrated the bitpack variant. Real Kimi command:

```sh

CUSTOM_KIMI_MOE_Q8=1 CUSTOM_KIMI_SHARED_Q8=1 CUSTOM_KIMI_ROUTER_TOPK=1 CUSTOM_KIMI_OUTPUT_ARGMAX_Q8=1 \
PYTHONPATH=. python3 tinygrad/llm/cli.py \
  --model /raid/gguf/kimi/Kimi-K2.6-UD-Q4_K_XL-00001-of-00014.gguf \
  --shard 3 --benchmark 8
```

## Selected Expert Down Grouped16

After xq staging, selected down was still around 2.9 ms/token. The 64-thread down kernel still had one CTA per output row, so every row reread the same `gate_up_q8` buffer. A grouped variant uses one 1024-thread CTA for 16 output rows, stages the 17KB `gate_up_q8` buffer once into shared memory, and keeps two warps per output row.

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 PROFILE=1 NO_COLOR=1 JITBEAM=2 PYTHONPATH=. ITERS=128 \
```

```text
grouped16                  about 37.2 us
```

```sh
python3 tinygrad/llm/cli.py \
  --model /raid/gguf/kimi/Kimi-K2.6-UD-Q4_K_XL-00001-of-00014.gguf \
  --shard 4 --benchmark 8
```

## Post-45 tok/s Rejected Routes

Current `PROFILE=1` stable decode budget after xq staging:

```text
tiny kernels          about 21.1 ms/token
selected gate/up      about  4.0 ms/token
selected down         about  2.9 ms/token
shared gate/up        about  1.4 ms/token
MoE input quant       about  0.55 ms/token
```

Shared expert grouped gate/up was tested with 32 rows per CTA and shared `xq` staging:

```text
current        836.58 us
grouped        860.89 us
```

Conclusion: unlike selected gate/up, shared expert gate/up does not benefit from 1024-thread grouped CTAs. The existing cache behavior is good enough and the grouped form is slower.

Attention Q8 row/grouped kernels were retested with activation quantization charged:

```text
attn_q_b:
generic                  226.13 us wall
q8 row quant+gemv        182.60 us wall
q8 grp quant+gemv        216.17 us wall

PROFILE kernel view:
generic                  about 18.6 us
q8 row                   about 16.1 us + 11.3 us quant

attn_output:
generic                  199.72 us wall
q8 row quant+gemv        154.06 us wall
q8 grp quant+gemv        204.01 us wall

PROFILE kernel view:
generic                  about 34.6 us
q8 row                   about 28.1 us + 12.7 us quant
```

Conclusion: raw Q8 attention GEMV kernels are faster by themselves, but per-layer activation quantization makes them slower than the existing generic path. Do not integrate attention Q8 unless quantization can be reused or avoided.

Output projection raw Q8 was also tested because `output.weight` is Q8_0 with shape `(7168, 163840)`:

```text
microbench:
generic argmax       713.88 us
q8 argmax            592.30 us

real Kimi with CUSTOM_KIMI_OUTPUT_Q8=1:
22.12 ms, 45.21 tok/s
22.04 ms, 45.38 tok/s
22.04 ms, 45.38 tok/s
22.05 ms, 45.35 tok/s

paired baseline without output Q8:
22.10 ms, 45.26 tok/s
22.05 ms, 45.35 tok/s
22.15 ms, 45.15 tok/s
22.05 ms, 45.34 tok/s
```

Conclusion: the standalone output microbench is positive, but real Kimi overlaps the baseline. The production code was reverted. This is not a confirmed decode-speed win.

Fused output Q8 argmax was tested next because greedy decode does not need to materialize all 163840 logits. The first prototype put stage1 and stage2 kernels in one custom source and faulted:

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 PROFILE=1 NO_COLOR=1 JITBEAM=2 PYTHONPATH=. ITERS=32 \
python3 /tmp/b1/bench_output_q8_argmax.py
```

Observed before failure:

```text
generic argmax       684.29 us
q8 logits            580.33 us
RuntimeError: MMU fault: 0x0 | NotPresent=0 ReadOnly=0 NoExecute=0 imprecise=0
```

Reduced repro showed small shapes also faulted when stage1 and stage2 were in the same source, then passed after splitting them into two independent custom-kernel sources:

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 JITBEAM=2 PYTHONPATH=. DIM=1024 VOCAB=4096 \
python3 /tmp/b1/bench_output_argmax_repro.py
```

With separate sources, full-shape microbench:

```text
generic argmax       689.62 us
q8 logits            573.96 us
q8 fused             513.72 us
```

Integrated behind:

```sh
CUSTOM_KIMI_OUTPUT_ARGMAX_Q8=1
```

Code changes:

- `tinygrad/llm/gguf.py`: preserve raw `output.weight` Q8_0 blocks only when `CUSTOM_KIMI_OUTPUT_ARGMAX_Q8=1`.
- `tinygrad/llm/amd_kimi.py`: add `kimi_output_argmax_q8_0(...)`, implemented as activation Q8 quantization plus two custom kernels:
  - stage1 computes 32 vocab rows per CTA and emits one `(value, index)` per group
  - stage2 reduces the 5120 group winners to one token id
- `tinygrad/llm/model.py`: for batch=1 decode on AMD with Kimi output shape `(163840, 7168)`, return the custom token id directly instead of materializing logits.

Real Kimi command:

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 JITBEAM=2 CUSTOM_KIMI_MOE_Q8=1 CUSTOM_KIMI_SHARED_Q8=1 CUSTOM_KIMI_ROUTER_TOPK=1 CUSTOM_KIMI_OUTPUT_ARGMAX_Q8=1 PYTHONPATH=. \
python3 tinygrad/llm/cli.py \
  --model /raid/gguf/kimi/Kimi-K2.6-UD-Q4_K_XL-00001-of-00014.gguf \
  --shard 4 --benchmark 8
```

```text
21.61 ms, 46.27 tok/s
21.68 ms, 46.12 tok/s
21.69 ms, 46.10 tok/s

run 2:
21.71 ms, 46.06 tok/s
21.64 ms, 46.20 tok/s
21.67 ms, 46.14 tok/s
21.68 ms, 46.13 tok/s
21.57 ms, 46.35 tok/s
```

Compared with grouped16 down around 45.7-45.9 tok/s, this is another small but repeatable decode improvement.

Fixed-shape RMSNorm 7168 was tested because `mean/square/rsqrt` still show up in the generic kernel bucket. A standalone custom kernel did the two-pass RMSNorm in one CTA:

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 PROFILE=1 NO_COLOR=1 JITBEAM=2 PYTHONPATH=. ITERS=1024 \
python3 /tmp/b1/bench_rmsnorm_7168.py
```

Result:

```text
generic         84.36 us
custom         204.96 us
```

Conclusion: do not integrate. Tinygrad's existing beam-generated RMSNorm expression is much faster than the naive single-CTA custom kernel for this shape.

Shared expert gate scalar was tested because every MoE layer computes:

```text
(x * ffn_gate_inp_shexp.weight).sum().sigmoid()
```

Standalone command:

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 PROFILE=1 NO_COLOR=1 JITBEAM=2 PYTHONPATH=. ITERS=2048 \
python3 /tmp/b1/bench_shexp_gate.py
```

```text

```

But PROFILE showed the custom kernel itself is slower:

```text
generic kernel r_256_7_4   about 11.6 us
custom bench_shexp_gate    about 17.6 us
```

Conclusion: do not integrate. The wall-time signal is not a real kernel win.

Output argmax group size was swept after the `CUSTOM_KIMI_OUTPUT_ARGMAX_Q8=1` win:

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 PROFILE=1 NO_COLOR=1 JITBEAM=2 PYTHONPATH=. ITERS=16 \
python3 /tmp/b1/bench_output_argmax_groups.py
```

```text
stage2 group8    about  18.9 us
stage2 group16   about  14.7 us
stage2 group32   about  12.3 us
```

Conclusion: keep production group size at 32. The small group8 wall-time edge is noise; kernel timings favor group32.

## Output Argmax XQ Staging

The output argmax stage1 kernel was still about 0.385 ms in a standalone PROFILE run. It runs 32 vocab rows per CTA, and each row warp rereads the same 7.6KB activation Q8 buffer. Tested staging that `xq` buffer into LDS once per CTA:

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 PROFILE=1 NO_COLOR=1 JITBEAM=2 PYTHONPATH=. ITERS=32 \
python3 /tmp/b1/bench_output_argmax_loadvec.py
```

Wall-time:

```text
pack           828.53 us
load32         822.13 us
pack_xstage    671.67 us
load32_xstage 1063.72 us
```

PROFILE kernel view:

```text
stage1 pack          0.385 ms
stage1 load32        0.367 ms
stage1 pack_xstage   0.313 ms
stage1 load32_xstage 0.500 ms
```

Integrated only the `pack_xstage` variant. Real Kimi `JITBEAM=6 --shard 3` with current best flags:

```text
run 1:
20.06 ms, 49.85 tok/s
20.08 ms, 49.81 tok/s
20.03 ms, 49.92 tok/s
20.06 ms, 49.86 tok/s
20.21 ms, 49.49 tok/s

run 2:
20.08 ms, 49.80 tok/s
20.04 ms, 49.91 tok/s
20.02 ms, 49.94 tok/s
20.02 ms, 49.95 tok/s
20.04 ms, 49.91 tok/s
```

Conclusion: this is a real but small speedup. It gets current best to about 49.95 tok/s, still just below stable 50 tok/s.

Follow-up last-mile variants:

```text
output xpack, ITERS=128:
wall xstage 665.14 us, xpack 665.27 us
PROFILE xstage 0.3051 ms, xpack 0.3046 ms

output stage2 warp reduction:
tree1024 13.545 us
warp1024 13.088 us
warp512  14.758 us
warp256  17.840 us
warp128  24.256 us

router warp topk:
current 20.752 us
warp512 20.527 us
warp256 22.509 us
warp128 24.176 us
```

Conclusion: do not integrate these. The output xpack result is effectively tied, stage2 only saves about 0.5 us/token, and router warp topk saves only about 0.2 us/layer.

2-GPU selected expert parallel was tested as a standalone topk split:

```text
single GPU: topk8 selected gate/up + down
2 GPUs:     topk4 on AMD + topk4 on AMD:1, then optional output reduce
```

PROFILE raw kernels suggested the per-GPU topk4 kernels are smaller, but the no-profile JIT wall-time was clearly worse:

```text
single_topk8       134.48 us
split_compute      250.39 us
split_reduce       263.64 us
```

Conclusion: even for selected MoE, where communication is only a tiny Q8 input plus a 7168-float output, the current tinygrad multi-device/JIT path does not turn 2-GPU expert split into batch=1 speedup. Do not start a production expert-parallel rewrite without first fixing the multi-device execution overhead.

After the 46 tok/s output-argmax path, the current profile still had selected down at about 2.66 ms/token:

```text
kimi_down_reduce_q4_q8_0   2.66 ms/token, 60 kernels, about 44 us/layer
```

Swept grouped down variants in:

```sh
python3 /tmp/b1/bench_down_grouped_sweep.py
```

PROFILE kernel averages:


No-PROFILE wall-time was noisy and kept most viable variants within about 1%, so the decision was made only after real Kimi runs. Production `kimi_down_reduce_q4_q8_0` was changed from `ROWS=16, WARPS_PER_ROW=2, THREADS=1024` to `ROWS=8, WARPS_PER_ROW=2, THREADS=512`.

```text
21.72 ms, 46.04 tok/s

21.69 ms, 46.10 tok/s
21.68 ms, 46.12 tok/s
```

Conclusion: this is a small but repeatable selected-down retune. It is not a route to 50 tok/s by itself, but it keeps the production path on the better side of the measured noise.

## Post-46 Shard 3 Recheck

After the selected-down 8x2 retune, rechecked `--shard 3` with all current best flags:

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 JITBEAM=2 \
CUSTOM_KIMI_MOE_Q8=1 CUSTOM_KIMI_SHARED_Q8=1 CUSTOM_KIMI_ROUTER_TOPK=1 CUSTOM_KIMI_OUTPUT_ARGMAX_Q8=1 \
PYTHONPATH=. python3 tinygrad/llm/cli.py \
  --model /raid/gguf/kimi/Kimi-K2.6-UD-Q4_K_XL-00001-of-00014.gguf \
  --shard 3 --benchmark 8
```

Two real runs:

```text
run 1:
21.53 ms, 46.44 tok/s
21.52 ms, 46.47 tok/s
21.41 ms, 46.71 tok/s
21.46 ms, 46.60 tok/s
21.42 ms, 46.68 tok/s

run 2:
21.55 ms, 46.41 tok/s
21.37 ms, 46.80 tok/s
21.43 ms, 46.67 tok/s
21.46 ms, 46.59 tok/s
21.39 ms, 46.75 tok/s
```

Compared with current `--shard 4` around 46.1-46.4 tok/s, `--shard 3` is the better current run configuration. This is a placement/configuration win, not a code change.

Checked `--shard 5` with the same flags:

```text
21.84 ms, 45.79 tok/s
21.64 ms, 46.21 tok/s
21.67 ms, 46.15 tok/s
21.67 ms, 46.15 tok/s
21.64 ms, 46.20 tok/s
```

Conclusion: `--shard 5` is slower than `--shard 3` and roughly in the same range as `--shard 4`; current recommendation stays `--shard 3`.

## Q8 Activation Quant Grouping

`kimi_quant_q8_0` still appears once per MoE layer plus output. Tested grouping multiple 32-element Q8 blocks per CTA:

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 PROFILE=1 NO_COLOR=1 JITBEAM=2 PYTHONPATH=. ITERS=4096 \
python3 /tmp/b1/bench_quant_q8_grouped.py
```

Wall-time:

```text
current      705.60 us
g2           686.09 us
g4           694.75 us
g7           702.33 us
g8           715.32 us
g14          674.39 us
g16          729.01 us
g28          747.99 us
g32          673.89 us
```

PROFILE kernel view:

```text
current kimi_quant_q8_0   11.14 us
g2                        11.33 us
g4                        11.21 us
g7                        11.13 us
g8                        11.31 us
g14                       11.27 us
g16                       11.25 us
g28                       11.07 us
g32                       11.15 us
```

Conclusion: do not integrate. The apparent wall-time gain is noise; grouping changes the quant kernel by only about 0.07 us at best.

## Q8 Activation Quant Rounding

Tested whether avoiding `v / d` or changing rounding in `kimi_quant_q8_0` helps. Variants used `v * (127 / m)` with `rintf`, manual round-to-nearest, and truncation:

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 PROFILE=1 NO_COLOR=1 JITBEAM=2 PYTHONPATH=. ITERS=4096 \
python3 /tmp/b1/bench_quant_round.py
```

Wall-time:

```text
current        704.08 us
rint_inv       691.10 us
manual         697.80 us
trunc          710.51 us
```

PROFILE kernel view:

```text
current kimi_quant_q8_0   11.356 us
rint_inv                  11.438 us
manual                    11.407 us
trunc                     11.499 us
```

Conclusion: do not integrate. The wall-time signal is noise; current kernel is still the fastest in PROFILE. Truncation also changes quantization semantics for no speed gain.

## Router TopK Thread Sweep

Current custom `kimi_router_topk` still costs about 20 us/layer. Tested lower-thread variants where each thread scans multiple experts before the CTA reduction:

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 PROFILE=1 NO_COLOR=1 JITBEAM=2 PYTHONPATH=. ITERS=2048 \
python3 /tmp/b1/bench_router_topk_sweep.py
```

Wall-time:

```text
generic        140.24 us
current         97.40 us
t512            97.98 us
t256            97.83 us
t128            97.72 us
t64            100.89 us
```

PROFILE kernel view:

```text
current kimi_router_topk   20.77 us
t512                       20.88 us
t256                       21.70 us
t128                       21.83 us
t64                        24.36 us
```

Conclusion: do not integrate. Reducing CTA width does not reduce the real topk cost; extra per-thread scanning and the same topk loop erase any smaller-reduction benefit.

## Selected Gate/Up Packed-Load Sweep

Tested whether replacing scalar byte loads in selected gate/up with unaligned 32-bit packed loads would help the Q4/Q8 sdot path:

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 PROFILE=1 NO_COLOR=1 JITBEAM=2 PYTHONPATH=. ITERS=128 \
python3 /tmp/b1/bench_gateup_loadvec.py
```

Wall-time looked slightly positive:

```text
current        916.93 us
w32            909.51 us
x32            907.10 us
w32x32         900.34 us
```

But PROFILE showed the packed-load kernels were not actually faster:

```text
current kimi_gate_up_q4_q8_to_q8_0   47.16 us
w32                                    48.38 us
x32                                    72.15 us
w32x32                                 69.87 us
```

Conclusion: do not integrate. The apparent wall gain was measurement noise; unaligned 32-bit loads from the Q4/Q8 block payload are worse than the scalar byte loads in the generated kernel.

## Selected Down XQ Prepack

After output argmax xq staging reached about 49.95 tok/s, revisited selected down. The bad `x32` packed-load variant was doing unaligned int loads from LDS. Tested instead pre-packing the staged `gate_up_q8` activation into aligned int words once per CTA:

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 PROFILE=1 NO_COLOR=1 JITBEAM=2 PYTHONPATH=. ITERS=256 \
python3 /tmp/b1/bench_down_xpack.py
```

Wall-time was noisy:

```text
current       1480.67 us
xpack         1490.87 us
```

PROFILE kernel view was positive:

```text
current kimi_down_reduce_q4_q8_0   33.58 us
xpack                               31.48 us
```

This is about 2.1 us per selected-down call. Real Kimi has 60 selected-down calls per token across the three active GPUs, so the expected win is about 0.12 ms/token.

Integrated the xpack variant. Real Kimi command:

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 JITBEAM=6 \
CUSTOM_KIMI_MOE_Q8=1 CUSTOM_KIMI_SHARED_Q8=1 CUSTOM_KIMI_ROUTER_TOPK=1 CUSTOM_KIMI_OUTPUT_ARGMAX_Q8=1 \
PYTHONPATH=. python3 tinygrad/llm/cli.py \
  --model /raid/gguf/kimi/Kimi-K2.6-UD-Q4_K_XL-00001-of-00014.gguf \
  --shard 3 --benchmark 8
```

Two real runs:

```text
run 1:
19.94 ms, 50.15 tok/s
19.90 ms, 50.26 tok/s
20.15 ms, 49.62 tok/s
19.93 ms, 50.17 tok/s
20.06 ms, 49.86 tok/s

run 2:
19.92 ms, 50.20 tok/s
19.90 ms, 50.25 tok/s
19.87 ms, 50.32 tok/s
19.88 ms, 50.31 tok/s
19.87 ms, 50.33 tok/s
```

Validation:

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 python3 -m ruff check tinygrad/llm/amd_kimi.py tinygrad/llm/model.py tinygrad/llm/gguf.py
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 python3 -m unittest test.unit.test_llm_moe
```

Conclusion: this is the first repeated run above 50 tok/s. Current best is about 50.3 tok/s with `JITBEAM=6 --shard 3`.

Follow-up `xpack_w32` test:

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 PROFILE=1 NO_COLOR=1 JITBEAM=2 PYTHONPATH=. ITERS=512 \
python3 /tmp/b1/bench_down_xpack.py
```


PROFILE kernel view:

```text

xpack                              31.515 us
xpack_w32                          31.186 us
```

Conclusion: do not integrate. The best kernel delta over current production is only about 0.55 us/call and wall-time regresses; this is too small for a trustworthy real decode speed commit.


Conclusion: this is the new best path, about 52.8-53.1 tok/s. Like gate/up, PROFILE kernel timing predicted the real decode gain better than microbench wall-time.

Current `PROFILE=1` stable decode budget after down bitpack:

```text
batched graph        about 28.0 ms/token in PROFILE mode
tiny kernels         about 19.1 ms/token in PROFILE mode
selected gate/up     about  3.22 ms/token
selected down        about  2.16 ms/token
shared gate/up       about  1.40 ms/token
MoE input quant      about  0.57 ms/token
```

Top custom/generic kernels in steady decode:

```text
kimi_gate_up_q4_q8_to_q8_0     about 1.07-1.08 ms/device for 20 layers
kimi_down_reduce_q4_q8_0       about 0.72 ms/device for 20 layers
r_192_32_16_4_2_3_2_4          about 0.67-0.72 ms/device
r_1792_4_16_2_2_16_2_4         about 0.67-0.72 ms/device
r_6_32_224_3_32                about 0.47-0.51 ms/device
kimi_shared_gate_up_q8_0       about 0.46-0.47 ms/device for 20 layers
kimi_router_topk               about 0.41 ms/device for 20 layers
```

Implication: further simple MoE kernel wins are getting small. The largest remaining practical direction is now generic graph/tiny-kernel reduction, or a larger layout/loader change such as true no-peak preshuffle.

### Gate/Up Interleaved Weight Recheck After Bitpack

The earlier Q4 gate/up preshuffle route had a clear kernel win before xq prepack and Q4 bitpack, but production loader integration failed and was memory-risky. Rechecked the microbench after the current bitpack kernel:

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 PROFILE=1 NO_COLOR=1 JITBEAM=2 PYTHONPATH=. ITERS=512 \
python3 /tmp/b1/bench_gateup_interleaved_bitpack.py
```

Wall-time:

```text
current          956.87 us
interleaved      825.51 us
```

PROFILE kernel view:

```text
current kimi_gate_up_q4_q8_to_q8_0   36.495 us
interleaved                          36.308 us
```

Conclusion: do not pursue the invasive loader/preshuffle route right now. After bitpack, the actual kernel delta is only about 0.2 us/call, far below what would justify a large no-peak interleaved loader rewrite.

## Attention F32 x Q8_0 GEMV

The earlier fixed-shape Q8 attention attempt lost once activation quantization was charged. Tested a no-activation-quant variant: `float activation x raw Q8_0 weight`, with the generic side built from `ggml_data_to_tensor(raw, ..., 8).cast(float16)` to match the loader path.

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 PROFILE=1 NO_COLOR=1 JITBEAM=2 PYTHONPATH=. ITERS=128 \
python3 /tmp/b1/bench_q8_attention_f32.py
```

Wall-time looked positive:

```text
attn_q_a generic wall 0.2073 ms
attn_q_a custom  wall 0.2035 ms
attn_kv_a_mqa generic wall 0.2266 ms
attn_kv_a_mqa custom  wall 0.2058 ms
attn_q_b generic wall 0.2131 ms
attn_q_b custom  wall 0.1987 ms
attn_output generic wall 0.5073 ms
attn_output custom  wall 0.2032 ms
```

But PROFILE kernel timings were negative:

```text
attn_q_a       generic 18.02 us   custom 20.72 us
attn_kv_a_mqa  generic 14.78 us   custom 15.36 us
attn_q_b       generic 19.92 us   custom 25.82 us
attn_output    generic 36.82 us   custom 51.45 us
```

Conclusion: do not integrate. Tinygrad's generated lazy-Q8/dequant GEMV is faster at the kernel level than the naive one-warp-per-row custom f32×Q8 path; wall-time here is dominated by scheduling/profile noise.

## Shared Expert Down Q8/F32 Recheck After 53 Tok/s

Rechecked shared expert down after the selected gate/up and selected down bitpack wins. A standalone chain benchmark compared the current production shared path against two custom down candidates:

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 PROFILE=1 NO_COLOR=1 JITBEAM=2 PYTHONPATH=.:/tmp/b1 ITERS=128 REPS=3 \
python3 /tmp/b1/bench_shared_current_vs_q8_chain.py
```

Standalone wall-time looked promising:

```text
current gate       median=  916.54 us
q8 gate            median=  916.50 us
current chain      median= 3944.37 us
q8 chain           median= 1511.97 us
```

Also rechecked f32 activation x Q8_0 down:

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 PROFILE=1 NO_COLOR=1 JITBEAM=2 PYTHONPATH=. ITERS=512 \
python3 /tmp/b1/bench_q8_shared_down_f32.py
```

```text
generic down       median= 2867.75 us
custom f32 down    median=  895.10 us
```

But real Kimi rejected both variants. Temporary production flags were tried and then reverted.

Q8 gate/up -> Q8 down:

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 JITBEAM=6 \
CUSTOM_KIMI_MOE_Q8=1 CUSTOM_KIMI_SHARED_Q8=1 CUSTOM_KIMI_SHARED_DOWN_Q8=1 CUSTOM_KIMI_ROUTER_TOPK=1 CUSTOM_KIMI_OUTPUT_ARGMAX_Q8=1 \
PYTHONPATH=. python3 tinygrad/llm/cli.py --model /raid/gguf/kimi/Kimi-K2.6-UD-Q4_K_XL-00001-of-00014.gguf --shard 3 --benchmark 8
```

```text
20.28 ms, 49.30 tok/s
20.14 ms, 49.65 tok/s
20.07 ms, 49.82 tok/s
20.07 ms, 49.83 tok/s
20.09 ms, 49.77 tok/s
```

PROFILE showed the reason: `kimi_shared_down_q8_0` was about 1.08 ms/token, but feeding it required `kimi_shared_gate_up_q8_to_q8_0` at about 2.43 ms/token, so the down win was spent on the new gate/up-to-Q8 path.

F32 gate/up -> custom Q8 down:

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 JITBEAM=6 \
CUSTOM_KIMI_MOE_Q8=1 CUSTOM_KIMI_SHARED_Q8=1 CUSTOM_KIMI_SHARED_DOWN_F32=1 CUSTOM_KIMI_ROUTER_TOPK=1 CUSTOM_KIMI_OUTPUT_ARGMAX_Q8=1 \
PYTHONPATH=. python3 tinygrad/llm/cli.py --model /raid/gguf/kimi/Kimi-K2.6-UD-Q4_K_XL-00001-of-00014.gguf --shard 3 --benchmark 8
```

```text
19.21 ms, 52.04 tok/s
19.25 ms, 51.95 tok/s
19.55 ms, 51.15 tok/s
19.26 ms, 51.91 tok/s
19.25 ms, 51.96 tok/s
```

Conclusion: do not integrate either shared-down variant. Standalone microbench wall-time is misleading here; the real decode graph stays slower than the current 52.8-53.1 tok/s path.

## Current-Best VIZ Profile And RMS Scale Rejection

Captured a fresh current-best VIZ/PROFILE run:

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 VIZ=1 PROFILE=1 NO_COLOR=1 JITBEAM=6 \
CUSTOM_KIMI_MOE_Q8=1 CUSTOM_KIMI_SHARED_Q8=1 CUSTOM_KIMI_ROUTER_TOPK=1 CUSTOM_KIMI_OUTPUT_ARGMAX_Q8=1 \
PYTHONPATH=. python3 tinygrad/llm/cli.py --model /raid/gguf/kimi/Kimi-K2.6-UD-Q4_K_XL-00001-of-00014.gguf --shard 3 --benchmark 8
```

Steady PROFILE decode was about 28.7 ms/token with profiling overhead. VIZ saved:

```text
/tmp/b1/profile.pkl.b1tg
/tmp/b1/rewrites.pkl.b1tg
```

The largest remaining generic kernels are still the MLA/attention Q8_0 GEMVs and small RMS/rope/cache work:

```text
r_192_32_16_4_2_3_2_4      attn_q_b, 1536 -> 12288 Q8_0 GEMV
r_1792_4_16_2_2_16_2_4     attn_output, 8192 -> 7168 Q8_0 GEMV + residual
r_6_32_224_3_32            attn_kv_a_mqa, 7168 -> 576 Q8_0 GEMV
r_4_16_28_4                7168-wide RMSNorm scale: rsqrt(mean(x^2)+eps)
```

Since the fixed-shape attention Q8 GEMV routes had already lost once quantization/real-model effects were charged, tested a narrower route: replace only the 7168-wide RMSNorm scalar reduction. This should preserve the existing fusion where later Q8_0 GEMVs consume `x * norm_weight * scale`.

Microbench:

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 PROFILE=1 NO_COLOR=1 JITBEAM=2 PYTHONPATH=. ITERS=512 REPS=5 \
python3 /tmp/b1/bench_rms_scale.py
```

Wall-time was too noisy to decide:

```text
generic        median=168.251 us
custom_t64     median=160.447 us
custom_t128    median=161.738 us
custom_t256    median=162.151 us
custom_t512    median=163.303 us
```

PROFILE kernel timing rejected the custom path:

```text
generic r_64_28_4        avg 11.382 us
custom_t64               avg 39.694 us
custom_t128              avg 23.558 us
custom_t256              avg 16.798 us
custom_t512              avg 13.520 us
```

Conclusion: do not integrate custom RMS scale. Tinygrad's generated reduction is already faster than the simple custom kernel; wall-time here is launch/profile noise.

## 2026-05-19 MLA Steady Decode Profile And Softmax-V Rejection

Reprofiled the current best Kimi path:

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 JITBEAM=6 PROFILE=1 \
CUSTOM_KIMI_MOE_Q8=1 CUSTOM_KIMI_SHARED_Q8=1 CUSTOM_KIMI_ROUTER_TOPK=1 CUSTOM_KIMI_OUTPUT_ARGMAX_Q8=1 \
PYTHONPATH=. python3 tinygrad/llm/cli.py \
  --model /raid/gguf/kimi/Kimi-K2.6-UD-Q4_K_XL-00001-of-00014.gguf \
  --shard 3 --benchmark 8
```

Profiler overhead run was stable around 28.4-28.7 ms/token. In steady decode 3-6, filtering to AMD kernel entries and averaging per token:

```text
AMD   total_avg 9.847 ms/token
AMD:1 total_avg 8.909 ms/token
AMD:2 total_avg 9.026 ms/token

Largest explicit buckets on AMD: selected gate/up 1.066 ms, selected down 0.723 ms,
attn q_b Q8 GEMV 0.710 ms, attn output Q8 GEMV 0.704 ms, attn kv_a Q8 GEMV 0.505 ms,
shared gate/up 0.464 ms, router topk 0.407 ms, shared down Q8 GEMV 0.353 ms.
```

The remaining large bucket is many small MLA/router/cache kernels:

```text
r_768_8_8_2_4_7_4                 0.34-0.36 ms/token
r_28start_pos2B129_4_16_4_2_2_36  0.24-0.26 ms/token
r_16_64_16_8_16_2                 0.24-0.26 ms/token
r_8_128_2_2_2_4_28start_pos2B129  0.20-0.22 ms/token
softmax max/sum/apply group        about 0.55-0.58 ms/token total
small RMS/elementwise kernels       about 0.16-0.21 ms/token each
```

Tested a standalone fused MLA `softmax(scores) @ v` kernel to see if removing the softmax max/sum/apply plus attn-v launches is a promising direction:

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 ITERS=512 REPS=5 PYTHONPATH=. \
python3 /tmp/b1/bench_mla_softmax_v.py
```

Results:

```text
S=1   generic 146.444 us, fused 147.185 us
S=8   generic  86.677 us, fused 147.816 us
S=32  generic  88.806 us, fused 151.400 us
S=128 generic  86.215 us, fused 148.037 us
```

Conclusion: reject this fused softmax-v shape. The simple one-kernel prototype repeats the softmax reduction for output chunks and is slower than the generated fixed-shape generic path. A production MLA fusion would need to fuse more of score generation + softmax + value projection in one design; just fusing softmax with `attn @ v` is not enough.

## 2026-05-19 RoPE Freqs Eager Realize Win

The MLA profile showed `E_8_32_8_16_8` in steady decode. Source inspection showed it computes the RoPE sin/cos table. `precompute_freqs_cis` was cached, but returned a lazy Tensor, so the freqs table could still enter decode graphs.

Changed:

```python
return freqs.cos().cat(freqs.sin(), dim=-1).contiguous().realize()
```

Validation:

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 python3 -m ruff check tinygrad/llm/model.py tinygrad/llm/amd_kimi.py tinygrad/llm/gguf.py
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 python3 -m unittest test.unit.test_llm_moe
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 JITBEAM=2 PYTHONPATH=. python3 tinygrad/llm/cli.py -m llama3.2:1b-q4 --benchmark 4
```

Ruff and unit tests passed. Llama 3.2 1B Q4 smoke loaded and completed decode; last benchmark token was 2.66 ms / 376.60 tok/s, with one compile spike on the previous token.

Real Kimi benchmark, same current-best flags:

```text
run 1: 18.59 ms 53.79 tok/s, 18.56 ms 53.88 tok/s, 18.50 ms 54.04 tok/s, 18.57 ms 53.86 tok/s, 18.54 ms 53.94 tok/s
run 2: 18.63 ms 53.67 tok/s, 18.62 ms 53.70 tok/s, 18.57 ms 53.86 tok/s, 18.57 ms 53.85 tok/s, 18.61 ms 53.73 tok/s
```

Previous repeated best was about 52.8-53.1 tok/s, so this is a reproducible ~1.3-1.8% improvement. PROFILE confirmed the intended mechanism:

```text
E_8_32_8_16_8 steady decode: 0 calls/token on AMD, AMD:1, AMD:2
Profiler-overhead decode improved from about 28.4-28.7 ms/token to about 28.0-28.4 ms/token.
```

Conclusion: keep this change. It removes repeated RoPE table generation from the decode graph and is the new best path, about 53.7-54.0 tok/s on `--shard 3`.

## 2026-05-19 REALIZE_SMALL Win

The post-RoPE profile still had repeated tiny kernels that were likely lazy casts or unpacking of small native parameters:

```text
E_128_3        about 0.18-0.19 ms/token before REALIZE_SMALL
E_56_16_4_2    about 0.19-0.20 ms/token before REALIZE_SMALL
```

Added an opt-in loader flag:

```python
if getenv("REALIZE_SMALL", 0):
  params = [p for p in nn.state.get_parameters(model) if p.numel() <= 1_000_000 and not hasattr(p, "_ggml_qtype")]
  for p in params: p.replace(p.contiguous())
  Tensor.realize(*params)
```

This materializes small non-quantized parameters after `load_state_dict`, without realizing huge quantized/dequantized weights.

Kimi benchmark with `REALIZE_SMALL=1` plus the current-best flags:

```text
run 1: 18.02 ms 55.49 tok/s, 17.89 ms 55.90 tok/s, 17.89 ms 55.90 tok/s, 17.91 ms 55.85 tok/s, 17.96 ms 55.68 tok/s
run 2: 18.02 ms 55.50 tok/s, 17.97 ms 55.64 tok/s, 17.91 ms 55.83 tok/s, 17.92 ms 55.80 tok/s, 17.96 ms 55.69 tok/s
```

PROFILE with `REALIZE_SMALL=1`:

```text
E_8_32_8_16_8   0 calls/token
E_128_3         0 calls/token
E_56_16_4_2     0 calls/token

AMD   total_avg 9.748 -> 9.345 ms/token
AMD:1 total_avg 8.908 -> 8.462 ms/token
AMD:2 total_avg 8.844 -> 8.656 ms/token
```

Validation:

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 python3 -m ruff check tinygrad/llm/model.py tinygrad/llm/amd_kimi.py tinygrad/llm/gguf.py
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 python3 -m unittest test.unit.test_llm_moe
```

Both passed.

Conclusion: keep `REALIZE_SMALL=1` as the new recommended flag. It raises the best repeated Kimi decode result from about 53.7-54.0 tok/s to about 55.5-55.9 tok/s.

Shard placement recheck with `REALIZE_SMALL=1`:

```text
--shard 4: 18.24 ms 54.83 tok/s, 18.02 ms 55.51 tok/s, 18.00 ms 55.55 tok/s, 17.98 ms 55.62 tok/s, 18.03 ms 55.46 tok/s
--shard 6: 18.43 ms 54.26 tok/s, 18.08 ms 55.32 tok/s, 18.08 ms 55.32 tok/s, 18.08 ms 55.32 tok/s, 18.10 ms 55.26 tok/s
```

Conclusion: keep `--shard 3`. Extra GPUs still do not help this layer-sharded path; 4 and 6 cards are slightly slower or tied below the best 3-card repeated result.

## 2026-05-20 REALIZE_SMALL Threshold Sweep

Changed `REALIZE_SMALL` to accept an element threshold while preserving old behavior: `REALIZE_SMALL=1` still means `1_000_000` elements. This lets Kimi materialize more small/medium non-quantized parameters without turning on full `REALIZE`.

Benchmarked with the current best flags and `--shard 3 --benchmark 8`:

```text
REALIZE_SMALL=1        previous repeated best about 55.5-55.9 tok/s
REALIZE_SMALL=2000000  18.06 ms 55.38, 17.89 ms 55.89, 17.86 ms 55.99, 17.90 ms 55.87, 17.93 ms 55.78
REALIZE_SMALL=4000000  17.97 ms 55.65, 17.84 ms 56.06, 17.88 ms 55.94, 17.78 ms 56.23, 17.81 ms 56.14
REALIZE_SMALL=8000000  17.58 ms 56.90, 17.56 ms 56.96, 17.57 ms 56.92, 17.52 ms 57.07, 17.56 ms 56.94
REALIZE_SMALL=8000000  17.80 ms 56.17, 17.58 ms 56.89, 17.53 ms 57.05, 17.55 ms 56.98, 17.56 ms 56.93
REALIZE_SMALL=12000000 17.59 ms 56.84, 17.63 ms 56.72, 17.52 ms 57.06, 17.60 ms 56.82, 17.51 ms 57.11
REALIZE_SMALL=16000000 17.91 ms 55.84, 17.65 ms 56.65, 17.59 ms 56.84, 17.60 ms 56.81, 17.68 ms 56.57
```

Conclusion: `REALIZE_SMALL=8000000` is the best confirmed point. `12M` ties it within noise while using more memory, and `16M` is slower. New recommended command uses `REALIZE_SMALL=8000000`, giving about `56.9-57.0 tok/s`.

## 2026-05-20 Output Argmax 2-Way TP Rejected

Tested a narrow tensor-parallel route that only splits final `output.weight` argmax by vocab rows across 2 GPUs. This looked more promising than attention GEMV TP because communication is small: one 7168-dim activation or Q8 activation to GPU1, then local best values/indices back to GPU0.

Standalone microbench `/tmp/b1/bench_output_argmax_tp.py`:

```text
ITERS=128: single 0.3719 ms, split2 0.3321 ms
ITERS=512: single 0.3560 ms, split2 0.3228 ms
```

PROFILE confirmed the isolated split can reduce graph time:

```text
single graph batched 3: 0.3274 ms avg
split2 graph batched 7: 0.2259 ms avg
single stage1:          0.3027 ms avg
split stage1 GPU0:      0.1590 ms avg
split stage1 GPU1:      0.1658 ms avg
copy Q8/partials:       about 0.005 ms each
```

Temporarily wired `CUSTOM_KIMI_OUTPUT_ARGMAX_TP=1` by sharding output raw rows `0:81920` on GPU0 and `81920:163840` on GPU1. The first real run had an apparent speed signal:

```text
17.01 ms 58.80, 17.03 ms 58.73, 16.98 ms 58.90, 17.03 ms 58.71, 17.02 ms 58.75
```

But the generated text diverged from the current baseline:

```text
baseline: [BOS] | `empp...
TP:       [BOS] | `emdoctor...
```

Debugging showed the raw TP attr initially was not propagated by `load_state_dict`; after fixing that temporarily, the model still diverged. Non-JIT checks on real Kimi output raw matched:

```text
random hidden: single [[92279]], tp [[92279]]
manual decode hidden, first 5 steps:
0 950 950 [BOS]
1 1268 1268 [BOS] |
2 426 426 [BOS] | `
3 1854 1854 [BOS] | `em
4 271 271 [BOS] | `empp
```

The failure is specific to TinyJit with cross-device TP output. `/tmp/b1/check_kimi_output_tp_jit.py` showed stale/wrong results after the first call:

```text
copy Q8 to GPU1 variant:
0 14126 14126
1 83661 11008
2 82378 83661
3 57002 83661
4 43698 43698

quantize separately on GPU1 variant:
0 161014 161014
1 153381 57999
2 129821 14728
3 1989 129821
4 137454 12128
```

Conclusion: reject and do not commit. The isolated TP output argmax has a real microbench speed signal, but cross-device work inside the rollout TinyJit is not correctness-safe. Production code was reverted.

## 2026-05-20 TP Proof-Of-Worth: Attention Output Q8 GEMV Rejected

Built `/tmp/b1/bench_tp_q8_gemv.py` to test whether a batch=1 Q8 GEMV can profitably split across 2 GPUs before doing any invasive Kimi tensor shard. Shape matches the MLA attention output GEMV: `8192 -> 7168`, Q8_0 raw weights.

The benchmark compares:

```text
single             one GPU computes full 8192 -> 7168
row_compute_only   output rows split 3584/3584, no gather
row_pre_copyback   output rows split, x already present on both GPUs, copy second half back and cat
row_isolated       output rows split, copy x to GPU1, copy second half back and cat
col_compute_only   input dim split 4096/4096, no reduce
col_reduce_pre     input dim split, x halves already present, copy partial output back and add
col_isolated       input dim split, slice/copy second x half, copy partial output back and add
```

Command:

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 ITERS=512 REPS=5 PYTHONPATH=. \
python3 /tmp/b1/bench_tp_q8_gemv.py
```

Results:

```text
single             median=152.382 us min=152.212 us
row_compute_only   median=158.026 us min=156.374 us
row_pre_copyback   median=169.064 us min=168.629 us
row_isolated       median=161.218 us min=160.372 us
col_compute_only   median=160.227 us min=159.333 us
col_reduce_pre     median=169.864 us min=169.369 us
col_isolated       median=169.026 us min=167.688 us
```

Correctness checks:

```text
diff 0.0
col_diff 0.0
```

PROFILE with `ITERS=128 REPS=2` explains the failure:

```text
single full kernel:              bench_tp_q8_gemv_8192_7168 avg about 0.030 ms
row half kernels:                8192_3584 on AMD/AMD:1 avg about 0.021-0.022 ms each
row compute-only graph:          batched 2 avg about 0.037 ms
row copyback graph:              batched 4 avg about 0.068 ms
row isolated graph:              batched 6 avg about 0.079 ms
column compute-only graph:       batched 2 avg about 0.038 ms
column reduce/copyback graph:    batched 4 avg about 0.073 ms
column isolated graph:           batched 6 avg about 0.078 ms
SDMA copy per small transfer:     about 0.004 ms, but extra graph and cat/add kernels dominate
```

Conclusion: no TP proof-of-worth for this representative attention GEMV. Even the compute-only 2-GPU upper bound is slower than single GPU, and realistic isolated TP with copy-back/reduce is clearly worse. This supports not doing full tensor shard now. A future TP attempt needs a larger fused region than one GEMV, so communication and graph overhead are amortized inside a full fused block.

## 2026-05-20 Attention Output Q8_0 Custom Path Rejected

Tested a fixed-shape Q8_0 path for MLA attention output, shape `8192 -> 7168`. The standalone script is `/tmp/b1/bench_q8_attention.py`.

Initial standalone wall-time without activation quantization included:

```text
attn_q_a generic 0.1465 ms, custom 0.1546 ms
attn_kv_a_mqa generic 0.1461 ms, custom 0.1535 ms
attn_q_b generic 0.1509 ms, custom 0.1541 ms
attn_output generic 0.3846 ms, custom 0.1552 ms
```

After adding activation quantization into the measured path, PROFILE still looked promising for `attn_output`:

```text
generic attn_output kernel r_56_32_4_2048_4 avg about 0.3756 ms
bench_quant_q8_0_8192 avg about 0.0122 ms
bench_q8_gemv_8192_7168 avg about 0.0294 ms
```

Integrated temporarily behind `CUSTOM_KIMI_ATTN_OUTPUT_Q8=1`. The first version put two extern kernels in one HIP source and hit a tinygrad HCQ kernargs metadata failure:

```text
ValueError: Array length must be >= 0, not -8
```

Splitting quant and GEMV into separate HIP sources fixed the minimal call test:

```text
/tmp/b1/test_attn_output_q8.py -> (1, 1, 7168)
```

Real Kimi A/B with `JITBEAM=6 REALIZE_SMALL=8000000 --shard 3 --benchmark 10`:

```text
CUSTOM_KIMI_ATTN_OUTPUT_Q8=1:
18.19 ms 54.97, 18.07 ms 55.34, 18.03 ms 55.46, 18.03 ms 55.48, 18.06 ms 55.39, 18.10 ms 55.25, 18.12 ms 55.19

baseline without CUSTOM_KIMI_ATTN_OUTPUT_Q8:
17.52 ms 57.08, 17.67 ms 56.61, 17.53 ms 57.04, 17.50 ms 57.13, 17.53 ms 57.05, 17.61 ms 56.78, 17.55 ms 56.98
```

Conclusion: reject and do not commit. The standalone kernel win does not survive the full decode graph; the extra quant/custom path loses about `1.5-2 tok/s` in real batch=1 decode. Production code was reverted.

## 2026-05-20 MLA Softmax+V+V_B Fusion Rejected

After the `JITBEAM=8` reprofile, the remaining repeated MLA kernels still included the softmax/attention-value path and `attn_v_b` projection. Tested a stronger fusion than the earlier `softmax(scores) @ v` prototype: one fixed-shape kernel computes `softmax(scores) @ v[:, :512]`, keeps the 512-wide intermediate in shared memory, then applies the per-head `v_b` projection to produce the 8192-wide attention output.

Script:

```sh
/tmp/b1/bench_mla_softmax_v_vb.py
```

Commands:

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 PROFILE=1 NO_COLOR=1 JITBEAM=2 PYTHONPATH=. ONLY=1 S=8 ITERS=128 REPS=3 \
python3 /tmp/b1/bench_mla_softmax_v_vb.py

TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 PROFILE=1 NO_COLOR=1 JITBEAM=2 PYTHONPATH=. ONLY=1 S=1 ITERS=128 REPS=3 \
python3 /tmp/b1/bench_mla_softmax_v_vb.py

TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 PROFILE=1 NO_COLOR=1 JITBEAM=2 PYTHONPATH=. ONLY=1 S=4 ITERS=128 REPS=3 \
python3 /tmp/b1/bench_mla_softmax_v_vb.py
```

Results:

```text
S=1 maxdiff 8.392e-05
generic_s1 median 241.489 us, min 240.244 us
fused_s1   median 248.055 us, min 242.894 us

S=4 maxdiff 4.768e-05
generic_s4 median 144.895 us, min 143.685 us
fused_s4   median 236.844 us, min 233.534 us

S=8 maxdiff 4.005e-05
generic_s8 median 148.543 us, min 147.142 us
fused_s8   median 241.283 us, min 238.932 us
```

Conclusion: reject and do not integrate. The fused kernel avoids launches, but one CTA per head serializes too much of the 512x128 `v_b` projection and loses badly for the short decode lengths that matter to `--benchmark`. A useful MLA fusion would need a different design that preserves the generated `v_b` projection parallelism while eliminating surrounding launches.

## 2026-05-20 Router Linear+TopK Fusion Rejected

Current profile after `JITBEAM=8` shows router work as two repeated kernels:

```text
r_384_16_8_7_8   router linear, about 13 us/layer in full Kimi profile
kimi_router_topk about 20 us/layer
```

Built `/tmp/b1/bench_router_linear_topk.py` to test a two-stage fused router:

- stage1: one CTA handles 16 experts, computes router GEMV + sigmoid + group top8
- stage2: reduce 24x8 group candidates to global top8 and normalize probs

This keeps the same two kernel launches as current `router GEMV + kimi_router_topk`, but avoids feeding topk with all 384 logits.

Command:

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 PROFILE=1 NO_COLOR=1 JITBEAM=2 PYTHONPATH=. ITERS=256 REPS=3 \
python3 /tmp/b1/bench_router_linear_topk.py
```

Correctness matched on random inputs:

```text
sel [285, 27, 361, 76, 14, 368, 236, 371] [285, 27, 361, 76, 14, 368, 236, 371]
diff 2.463307335887066e-16
```

Wall-time:

```text
current median 114.472 us, min 111.755 us
fused   median 150.075 us, min 149.894 us
```

PROFILE kernel timing explains the loss:

```text
current router linear r_384_256_4_7   15.5 us
current kimi_router_topk              19.6 us
fused stage1                          80.1 us
fused stage2                          18.4 us
```

Conclusion: reject and do not integrate. Reducing the topk candidate count is not enough; the grouped stage1 router GEMV is far slower than tinygrad's generated one-CTA-per-expert router linear. The existing router path should stay as generated linear + custom topk.

## Next Plan

1. Current stable run target is already above 50 tok/s: `JITBEAM=8 REALIZE_SMALL=8000000 --shard 3` with `CUSTOM_KIMI_MOE_Q8=1 CUSTOM_KIMI_SHARED_Q8=1 CUSTOM_KIMI_ROUTER_TOPK=1 CUSTOM_KIMI_OUTPUT_ARGMAX_Q8=1`, about 58.1-58.5 tok/s.
2. Keep shared expert down on the generic path. Both Q8-chain and f32-activation custom down looked good standalone, but lost in real Kimi.
3. Keep attention output on the generic path. The fixed-shape Q8_0 custom path looked good standalone but was slower in real Kimi.
4. The next credible target is generic graph/tiny-kernel reduction: identify the remaining `r_*` kernels in steady decode and remove/fuse only if the full decode graph improves.
5. Do not integrate full Q4 gate/up preshuffle unless the loader can avoid the separate raw + interleaved raw peak.
6. Do not revisit partial TP as a whole-block design. Only consider isolated large GEMV groups after a single-GPU kernel has a clear per-call win and a small copy/sync surface.

Current working hypothesis:

- The 53 tok/s path is no longer dominated by one obvious selected-expert Q4 read. Selected gate/up, selected down, shared gate/up, router topk, and output argmax have all had their easy wins taken.
- Standalone GEMV wall-time is now too weak a signal. PROFILE kernel timing plus real Kimi `--benchmark` is the decision rule.

```sh
python3 /tmp/b1/bench_mla_weight_layout.py
```

Wall timing:

```text

kb_pre_f32           210.48 us
kb_pre_f16           208.50 us
vb_current_q8        218.54 us
vb_pre_f32           211.50 us
vb_pre_f16           210.94 us
```

PROFILE showed a real kernel-shape change, especially for `vb_pre_f16`, so a guarded production experiment was tried. The first lazy-realize attempt failed because `@function`/JIT trace disallows AMD buffer realization inside `_attention`:

```text
AssertionError: usage of device AMD disallowed
```

Then tested a load-time shape-transform variant under `CUSTOM_KIMI_MLA_BT_F16=1`, where the model expects pretransposed `attn_k_b/attn_v_b` and `from_gguf` transposes those state_dict entries before load. Full decode command:

```sh

CUSTOM_KIMI_MLA_BT_F16=1 CUSTOM_KIMI_MOE_Q8=1 CUSTOM_KIMI_SHARED_Q8=1 CUSTOM_KIMI_ROUTER_TOPK=1 CUSTOM_KIMI_OUTPUT_ARGMAX_Q8=1 \

  --model /raid/gguf/kimi/Kimi-K2.6-UD-Q4_K_XL-00001-of-00014.gguf \
  --shard 3 --benchmark 8
```

Observed steady decode:

```text
17.56 ms, 56.96 tok/s
17.32 ms, 57.73 tok/s
17.25 ms, 57.96 tok/s
17.25 ms, 57.98 tok/s
17.31 ms, 57.79 tok/s
```

Conclusion: reject and revert. The microbench looked promising, but full Kimi decode did not beat the current repeated best `JITBEAM=8 REALIZE_SMALL=8000000 --shard 3` result around `58.1-58.5 tok/s`. No code change and no commit.

## 2026-05-20 Output Head On Last Shard Device Rejected

Tested a layer-shard placement idea: with `--shard 3`, block layers end on GPU2, but non-block weights default to GPU0, so decode moves the final hidden state back to GPU0 for `output_norm` and output argmax. The experiment moved `output.weight`, `output.weight._ggml_raw`, and `output_norm.weight` to the last shard device, with a scalar token move back to the embedding device at the next step.

Full decode command:

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 JITBEAM=8 REALIZE_SMALL=8000000 \
CUSTOM_KIMI_OUTPUT_LAST_DEVICE=1 CUSTOM_KIMI_MOE_Q8=1 CUSTOM_KIMI_SHARED_Q8=1 CUSTOM_KIMI_ROUTER_TOPK=1 CUSTOM_KIMI_OUTPUT_ARGMAX_Q8=1 \
PYTHONPATH=. python3 tinygrad/llm/cli.py \
  --model /raid/gguf/kimi/Kimi-K2.6-UD-Q4_K_XL-00001-of-00014.gguf \
  --shard 3 --benchmark 8
```

Observed:

```text
43.07 ms, 23.22 tok/s
42.34 ms, 23.62 tok/s
42.31 ms, 23.64 tok/s
42.33 ms, 23.62 tok/s
42.41 ms, 23.58 tok/s
```

Conclusion: reject and revert. Avoiding the final hidden-state copy is not enough; moving the output head to the last shard device badly disrupts the current rollout graph/device flow and halves decode speed. No code change and no commit.

## 2026-05-20 GPU Topology Subset Check

Checked whether choosing a different 3-GPU subset is likely to help layer-shard decode.

Command:

```sh
rocm-smi --showtopo
```

Relevant result:

```text
All GPU pairs report XGMI, 1 hop, weight 15.
GPU0-GPU3 numa node 0.
GPU4-GPU7 numa node 1.
```

Conclusion: do not spend full Kimi runs on random GPU subsets right now. The current default `AMD:0,1,2` already stays within the same NUMA node and the XGMI topology is uniform across pairs. A different subset is unlikely to explain a `58 tok/s -> 60+ tok/s` jump.

## 2026-05-20 MLA Split Cache Rejected

Tested a Kimi MLA layout change: instead of caching `c_kv.cat(k_rope)` and computing one 576-wide score matmul, keep two caches and compute:

```text
score = q_nope_projected @ c_kv_cache.T + q_rope @ rope_cache.T
```

This avoids the 576-wide cache cat/read shape, but adds a second score matmul and add.

Full decode command:

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 JITBEAM=8 REALIZE_SMALL=8000000 \
CUSTOM_KIMI_MLA_SPLIT_CACHE=1 CUSTOM_KIMI_MOE_Q8=1 CUSTOM_KIMI_SHARED_Q8=1 CUSTOM_KIMI_ROUTER_TOPK=1 CUSTOM_KIMI_OUTPUT_ARGMAX_Q8=1 \
PYTHONPATH=. python3 tinygrad/llm/cli.py \
  --model /raid/gguf/kimi/Kimi-K2.6-UD-Q4_K_XL-00001-of-00014.gguf \
  --shard 3 --benchmark 6
```

Observed steady decode:

```text
18.42 ms, 54.28 tok/s
18.31 ms, 54.62 tok/s
18.22 ms, 54.90 tok/s
```

Conclusion: reject and revert. The extra score matmul/add costs more than the cache-cat/layout simplification saves. This is clearly slower than the current repeated best around `58.1-58.5 tok/s`. No code change and no commit.

## 2026-05-20 MoE Packed Intermediate Prototype Incomplete

Tested a selected-MoE dataflow change where gate/up writes the exact packed Q8 intermediate layout consumed by down, instead of producing Q8 bytes and having down repack them again inside every CTA.

Script:

```sh
/tmp/b1/bench_moe_packed_intermediate.py
```

Command:

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 PROFILE=1 NO_COLOR=1 JITBEAM=2 PYTHONPATH=. ITERS=128 \
python3 /tmp/b1/bench_moe_packed_intermediate.py
```

Partial result before fault:

```text
experts=384 gate_each=3.17 GB down=3.17 GB sel=[0, 54, 108, 162, 216, 270, 324, 383]
gate_current       930.59 us
gate_xpack        1024.97 us
down_current      1640.75 us
RuntimeError: MMU fault: 0x789633AEE000 | NotPresent=0 ReadOnly=0 NoExecute=0 imprecise=0
```

Conclusion so far: no integration and no commit. The first packed gate/up version is already slower than production before measuring down, and `down_xpack` must be isolated with a smaller test before this route can be judged safely.

Follow-up isolation:

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 NO_COLOR=1 JITBEAM=2 PYTHONPATH=. EXPERTS=8 ITERS=4 \
python3 /tmp/b1/bench_moe_packed_intermediate.py

TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 NO_COLOR=1 JITBEAM=2 PYTHONPATH=. MODE=down_direct ZERO=1 ZERO_DOWN=1 PROBE=1 EXPERTS=8 ITERS=4 \
python3 /tmp/b1/bench_moe_packed_intermediate.py
```

Results:

```text
EXPERTS=8 full path:
gate_current       867.20 us
gate_xpack         945.97 us
down_current      1358.03 us
down_xpack        MMU fault

down_direct with zero xps/xss/down_w and probe kernel:
bench_down_probe  MMU fault
```

Conclusion: reject this specific packed-intermediate prototype. The packed gate/up is slower, and even a same-parameter probe launch faults, so this is not a usable proof of speed. Do not commit. A future version would need to be rebuilt from the known-good production down kernel, not patched from this prototype.

Addendum: the fault was caused by the temporary script putting multiple HIP kernels in one compiled source. tinygrad AMD currently reads the kernel descriptor from the beginning of `.rodata`, so the first kernel's metadata can be used for later kernel symbols. Splitting `bench_down_from_xpack` into its own source fixed the MMU fault.

Retest after single-kernel-source split:

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 NO_COLOR=1 JITBEAM=2 PYTHONPATH=. EXPERTS=384 ITERS=64 \
python3 /tmp/b1/bench_moe_packed_intermediate.py
```

Result:

```text
gate_current       825.13 us
gate_xpack         923.98 us
down_current      1359.92 us
down_xpack        1360.51 us
chain_current     1904.46 us
chain_xpack       2003.95 us
```

Final conclusion: reject. The fixed prototype runs, but the packed intermediate chain is about `5%` slower. No code change and no commit.

## 2026-05-20 Custom Kernel Descriptor Audit

After the packed-intermediate fault, audited `/tmp/b1/bench_*.py` files with more than one `extern "C" __global__` in a file. The actual hazard is not multiple kernels per Python file; it is compiling a single HIP source containing multiple kernels and then launching a later symbol, because tinygrad AMD currently reads the descriptor from the beginning of `.rodata`.

Checked the main multi-stage scripts:

```text
bench_output_q8_argmax.py              stage sources are sliced before compile
bench_output_argmax_repro.py           stage sources are sliced before compile
bench_output_argmax_xstage_groups.py   stage1/stage2 are separate compiled strings
bench_output_argmax_groups.py          stage1/stage2 are separate compiled strings
bench_output_argmax_tp.py              stage1/stage2 are separate generated strings
bench_output_stage2_reduce.py          variant source contains one kernel per compile
bench_q8_attention.py                  quant/gemv are separate generated strings
bench_q8_grouped_attention.py          row/grouped GEMV are separate generated strings
bench_q8_shared_down.py                quant/down are separate source strings
bench_q8_shared_e2e.py                 gate/down are separate source strings
bench_router_linear_topk.py            stage1/stage2 are separate source strings
```

Conclusion: only `/tmp/b1/bench_moe_packed_intermediate.py` had a confirmed descriptor mismatch in the tested path, and it has been retested after splitting `down_xpack` into a single-kernel source. No earlier speed commit or rejected route needs to be overturned solely because of this issue.

## 2026-05-20 Selected Gate/Up Split-K Rejected

AITER source suggests split-K for small-token MoE GEMM paths, so tested a Q4_0-specific proof-of-worth for our selected gate/up kernel.

Current tinygrad selected gate/up already launches:

```text
TOPK * (HIDDEN / 32) = 8 * 64 = 512 CTAs
each CTA has 1024 threads = 32 row warps
each row warp scans DIM/32 = 224 Q4 blocks
```

Prototype:

- stage1: split K into 2 halves, compute partial gate/up sums into `[2, topk, hidden, 2]` float buffer
- stage2: reduce the two partials, apply SiLU*up, quantize to Q8_0

Script:

```sh
/tmp/b1/bench_gateup_splitk.py
```

Commands:

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 PROFILE=1 NO_COLOR=1 JITBEAM=2 PYTHONPATH=. EXPERTS=8 ITERS=16 \
python3 /tmp/b1/bench_gateup_splitk.py

TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 PROFILE=1 NO_COLOR=1 JITBEAM=2 PYTHONPATH=. EXPERTS=384 ITERS=64 \
python3 /tmp/b1/bench_gateup_splitk.py
```

Results:

```text
EXPERTS=8:
current        918.83 us
split2        1928.41 us

EXPERTS=384:
current        909.05 us
split2        1971.16 us
```

Conclusion: reject. The extra launch plus partial write/read dominates. For this Q4_0 fused gate/up+SiLU+Q8 path, split-K does not have proof-of-worth. No code change and no commit.

## 2026-05-20 MoE Expert-Parallel Full FFN Proof Rejected

Tested the highest-upside remaining TP-like route: split the whole selected MoE FFN across two GPUs by expert group instead of splitting one GEMV.

Prototype:

```sh
/tmp/b1/bench_moe_ep_full.py
```

Shape:

- single baseline: local topk=8 selected FFN on GPU0
- split: topk=4 selected FFN on GPU0 plus topk=4 selected FFN on GPU1
- each local FFN runs the full current path: `gate/up Q4 x Q8 -> Q8 intermediate -> down Q4 x Q8`
- `split_pre`: Q8 activation and local selector/probs already present on GPU1
- `split_realistic`: copy Q8 activation plus tiny selector/probs to GPU1, then copy/reduce the 7168-wide output back to GPU0
- local expert weights are independent local buffers, matching real expert placement better than a slice/view of a full tensor

Correctness sanity with zero-initialized weights:

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 DEV=AMD PYTHONPATH=. ZERO=1 ITERS=128 REPS=5 \
python3 /tmp/b1/bench_moe_ep_full.py
```

Result:

```text
diff_pre 0.0
single_topk8       median=  130.77 us
topk4_dev0         median=  129.75 us
topk4_dev1         median=  128.99 us
split_compute      median=  247.27 us
split_pre          median=  260.39 us
split_realistic    median=  260.42 us
```

Speed run with uninitialized finite/invalid values ignored, timing only:

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 DEV=AMD PYTHONPATH=. ITERS=128 REPS=5 \
python3 /tmp/b1/bench_moe_ep_full.py
```

Result:

```text
single_topk8       median=  124.28 us
topk4_dev0         median=  125.14 us
topk4_dev1         median=  123.33 us
split_compute      median=  239.87 us
split_pre          median=  253.02 us
split_realistic    median=  252.11 us
```

DEBUG=2 kernel view:

```text
single topk8 kernels:
gateup topk8 ~44.9 us
down topk8   ~36.2 us
captured batched-2 steady ~72 us

split compute kernels:
gateup topk4 ~25-30 us on each GPU
down topk4   ~19-23 us on each GPU
captured batched-4 steady ~59-78 us

split with reduce/copy:
28 KB GPU1 -> GPU0 copy ~118-144 us before graph capture
captured batched-6/9 steady roughly ~60-131 us, not a stable win over single
```

Conclusion: reject. The ideal compute-only graph is at best within noise of single GPU, and the realistic route is slower once the 7168-wide reduce/copy and multi-device graph overhead are included. This does not meet the proof-of-worth bar for production expert-parallel weight placement. No commit.

## 2026-05-20 HCQ Copy Queue Sweep Rejected

Since the current best uses `--shard 3`, tested whether HCQ graph copy-queue settings improve the inter-GPU layer-shard transfers. Source check:

```text
tinygrad/runtime/graph/hcq.py:
self.num_copy_queues = getenv("HCQ_NUM_SDMA", min(len(self.devices), 8) if ALL2ALL >= 1 else 1)
```

So:

- default: one SDMA copy queue
- `ALL2ALL=1`: uses up to one queue per graph device
- `HCQ_NUM_SDMA=2`: directly forces two SDMA copy queues
