## 2026-05-20 Schedule Linearization Knobs Rejected

Checked remaining schedule/codegen knobs that can change generated graph/kernel structure.

Source notes:

```text
tinygrad/schedule/indexing.py:
PCONTIG changes when multi-consumer ranges are merged or partially realized.

tinygrad/schedule/rangeify.py:
PCONTIG>2 enables more aggressive bufferize removal/local partial-contiguous rewrites.

tinygrad/codegen/late/linearizer.py:
TUPLE_ORDER changes the tie-breaker used while ordering UOps for linearization.
```

`PCONTIG=1` with current best flags failed during first-token compilation:

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 PCONTIG=1 JITBEAM=8 REALIZE_SMALL=8000000 \
CUSTOM_KIMI_MOE_Q8=1 CUSTOM_KIMI_SHARED_Q8=1 CUSTOM_KIMI_ROUTER_TOPK=1 CUSTOM_KIMI_OUTPUT_ARGMAX_Q8=1 \
PYTHONPATH=. python3 tinygrad/llm/cli.py \
  --model /raid/gguf/kimi/Kimi-K2.6-UD-Q4_K_XL-00001-of-00014.gguf \
  --shard 3 --benchmark 8
```

Observed failure:

```text
29747.73 ms, 0.03 tok/s, 0.00 GB/s, 3/584090 MB -- [BOS]
tinygrad.device.CompileError: comgr fail 1, ERROR
```

`PCONTIG=3` was not run after this. It is a more aggressive variant of the same schedule path (`PCONTIG` truthy plus `PCONTIG>2`), so the less aggressive variant already failing codegen is enough to reject this route for Kimi decode.

`TUPLE_ORDER=0`:

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 TUPLE_ORDER=0 JITBEAM=8 REALIZE_SMALL=8000000 \
CUSTOM_KIMI_MOE_Q8=1 CUSTOM_KIMI_SHARED_Q8=1 CUSTOM_KIMI_ROUTER_TOPK=1 CUSTOM_KIMI_OUTPUT_ARGMAX_Q8=1 \
PYTHONPATH=. python3 tinygrad/llm/cli.py \
  --model /raid/gguf/kimi/Kimi-K2.6-UD-Q4_K_XL-00001-of-00014.gguf \
  --shard 3 --benchmark 8
```

Observed steady decode:

```text
17.33 ms, 57.69 tok/s
17.26 ms, 57.95 tok/s
17.18 ms, 58.21 tok/s
17.23 ms, 58.04 tok/s
17.25 ms, 57.99 tok/s
```

Conclusion: reject. `PCONTIG=1` is not a valid Kimi decode configuration due compile failure, and `TUPLE_ORDER=0` does not beat the current `58.1-58.5 tok/s` best. Keep defaults: `PCONTIG=0`, `TUPLE_ORDER=1`. No commit.

## 2026-05-20 HCQ2 Not Tested For Kimi Shard

Checked whether `HCQ2=1` is a plausible route for the current `--shard 3` Kimi decode.

Source evidence:

```text
tinygrad/engine/realize.py:
if getenv("HCQ2"):
  from extra.hcq2.hcq2 import pm_hcq_exec
  pm_exec = pm_hcq_exec + pm_exec

extra/hcq2/graph/hcq.py:
return new_call.src[0].op in (Ops.PROGRAM, Ops.COPY) and len(all_devs) == 1 and isinstance(all_devs[0], HCQ2Compiled)
```

The current recommended Kimi path is explicitly multi-device (`--shard 3`) and uses layer-shard GPU-to-GPU transfers. HCQ2 graph batching only supports a single device per batched graph item, so this is not a direct replacement for the current multi-device graph path.

Conclusion: do not spend a full Kimi run on `HCQ2=1` for the current target. It is a backend rewrite route, not a low-risk tuning route, and the source shows it does not directly cover the multi-device layer-sharded graph shape we are optimizing. No commit.

## 2026-05-20 Remaining Runtime Knobs Source Audit

Audited the remaining tinygrad runtime/codegen knobs before spending more full Kimi runs. The target is steady batch=1 decode speed, not prefill, compile time, or debug/profile behavior.

Source evidence:

```text
tinygrad/codegen/__init__.py:
to_program cache key = (NOOPT, DEVECTORIZE, EMULATED_DTYPES, NOLOCALS, USE_TC, IMAGE, DISABLE_FAST_IDIV, TRANSCENDENTAL, ALLOW_TF32)

tinygrad/codegen/__init__.py:
if SPEC: type_verify(ast, spec_tensor)
if SPEC: type_verify(lst, spec_program)

tinygrad/codegen/opt/search.py:
if not disable_cache and CACHELEVEL >= 1 and diskcache_get("beam_search", key) is not None: ...

tinygrad/device.py:
Compiler.__init__: self.cachekey = cachekey if CCACHE else None

tinygrad/codegen/opt/postrange.py:
if self.ren.target.device in ("CUDA", "NV") and tc.dtype_in == dtypes.float and not ALLOW_TF32: continue

tinygrad/schedule/allreduce.py:
handle_allreduce returns None unless buf.device is a tuple

tinygrad/schedule/multi.py:
ALLREDUCE_CAST and LATE_ALLREDUCE only rewrite Ops.ALLREDUCE

tinygrad/schedule/memory.py:
if NO_MEMORY_PLANNER: return linear

tinygrad/codegen/late/devectorizer.py:
if getenv("UNSAFE_DISABLE_MASK", 0): vec = vec.get_idx()

tinygrad/runtime/ops_null.py:
NULL_ALLOW_COPYOUT only affects NULL backend copyout
```

Conclusions:

- `SPEC=0`: skips verifier work during schedule/codegen, but `SPEC` is not in `to_program`'s generated-program cache key and does not change the AMD kernel source. It can reduce compile overhead, not steady decoded token time.
- `CCACHE=0` and `CACHELEVEL=0`: disable compiler/beam disk caches. These can only make compile/prewarm worse for our cached current-best path; they do not provide a new steady kernel.
- `ALLOW_TF32=1`: the relevant guard is CUDA/NV-only, so it is a no-op for MI300X/AMD.
- `RING`, `ALL2ALL`, `ALLREDUCE_CAST`, `LATE_ALLREDUCE`: only affect `Ops.ALLREDUCE`. Current layer-shard Kimi path uses sequential device placement and copies, not tensor-parallel allreduce.
- `NULL_ALLOW_COPYOUT`: NULL backend only, irrelevant to AMD.
- `NO_MEMORY_PLANNER=1`: affects buffer reuse/lifetimes, not kernel math; expected to increase memory pressure and does not target a top decode bucket.
- `UNSAFE_DISABLE_MASK=1`: explicitly unsafe indexing-mask removal. This is not a correctness-safe route for Kimi.
- `CAPTURE_PROCESS_REPLAY`: records rewrite inputs for debugging and does not change the generated steady kernels.

Conclusion: reject this batch as low-value/no-op/debug-only for the current goal. The remaining credible speed work must change a real top decode kernel or graph dataflow; runtime/debug knobs no longer look like a path to 50+ tok/s. No commit.

## 2026-05-20 NOOPT Source Audit

Checked `NOOPT=1` because it is in `to_program`'s cache key and had not been separately recorded.

Source evidence:

```text
tinygrad/codegen/opt/postrange.py:
if ast.arg is not None and ast.arg.opts_to_apply is not None:
  ...
elif beam >= 1:
  k = beam_search(k, rawbufs, beam, ...)
elif not NOOPT and (ast.arg is None or ast.arg.applied_opts == ()):
  k = hand_coded_optimizations(k)
```

The current recommended command uses `JITBEAM=8`, so generated kernels take the `beam >= 1` branch. In that branch `NOOPT` does not disable beam search and does not create a new optimization policy. Without beam, `NOOPT=1` would only skip hand-coded optimizations, which is not a plausible speed route for this already beam-tuned Kimi path.

Conclusion: do not spend a full Kimi run on `NOOPT=1`. It is either a no-op under `JITBEAM=8` or a de-optimization when beam is disabled. No commit.

## 2026-05-20 IMAGE And EMULATED_DTYPES Source Audit

Checked the last `to_program` cache-key knobs not otherwise covered by real Kimi runs.

Source evidence:

```text
tinygrad/codegen/__init__.py:
if IMAGE and ren.target.device in {"QCOM", "CL", "PYTHON", "NULL"}:
  sink = graph_rewrite(sink, pm_make_images, ...)

tinygrad/codegen/opt/heuristic.py:
if IMAGE:
  ... ImageDType.valid_dims(...)

tinygrad/uop/symbolic.py:
if IMAGE.value > 0 and x.op_in_backward_slice_with_self(DIV/MOD): return None

tinygrad/uop/decompositions.py:
def _should_emulate(dt):
  return dt in EMULATED_DTYPES.tolist(dtypes) or not is_dtype_supported(dt, target)
```

Interpretation:

- `IMAGE=1` is intended for image-buffer backends and conv/dot image lowering. The explicit image-buffer rewrite is gated to QCOM/CL/PYTHON/NULL, not AMD. On AMD it can still perturb symbolic simplification and heuristic upcasting through global `IMAGE`, but that is an indirect de-optimization risk rather than a plausible path to faster Kimi decode.
- `EMULATED_DTYPES` forces dtype decomposition for dtypes that are otherwise supported. Kimi decode on MI300X depends heavily on supported half/float/int8/uint8 paths and custom raw Q4/Q8 kernels. Forcing emulation would add decompositions, not expose a faster hardware path.

Conclusion: do not spend full Kimi runs on `IMAGE=1` or `EMULATED_DTYPES=...`. They are backend/dtype-emulation controls, not credible speed routes for the current AMD Kimi decode path. No commit.

## 2026-05-20 Real GGUF Q8 GEMV TP Microbench

Geohot pushed back on the earlier "batch=1 GEMV shard does not help" conclusion. Built a narrower microbench that loads one real Kimi Q8_0 tensor directly from the GGUF split and compares one-GPU GEMV with 2/4 GPU row shard. This avoids synthetic weights and avoids full-model load.

Script:

```sh
/tmp/b1/bench_real_gguf_q8_tp.py
```

Per-layer MLA output tensor, `blk.1.attn_output.weight`, shape `(8192, 7168)`, raw Q8_0 size about 60 MB:

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 ITERS=256 REPS=3 PYTHONPATH=. \
python3 /tmp/b1/bench_real_gguf_q8_tp.py
```

Result:

```text
diff2 0.0
diff4 0.0
single        153.715 us
tp2_compute   159.170 us
tp2_gather    170.525 us
tp2_isolated  165.219 us
tp4_compute   304.477 us
tp4_gather    328.408 us
tp4_isolated  270.635 us
```

PROFILE/VIZ for the same tensor:

```text
single kernel real_gguf_q8_gemv_8192_7168: 30.63 us avg
tp2 compute graph, two 3584-row kernels: 37.79 us avg
tp4 compute graph, four 1792-row kernels: 48.32 us avg
tp2 gather graph: 50.27 us avg
tp4 gather graph: 60.97 us avg
```

Conclusion for this per-layer GEMV: row sharding does not help. Half/quarter kernels individually get smaller, but the multi-device graph time is already above the single full kernel, and gather/copy makes it worse.

Huge output tensor, `output.weight`, shape `(7168, 163840)`, raw Q8_0 size about 1.25 GB:

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 TENSOR_NAME=output.weight ITERS=32 REPS=3 PYTHONPATH=. \
python3 /tmp/b1/bench_real_gguf_q8_tp.py
```

Result:

```text
diff2 0.0
diff4 0.0
single        517.667 us
tp2_compute   339.277 us
tp2_gather    339.633 us
tp2_isolated  407.621 us
tp4_compute   313.908 us
tp4_gather    347.550 us
tp4_isolated  509.821 us
```

PROFILE/VIZ:

```text
single kernel real_gguf_q8_gemv_7168_163840: 526 us avg
tp2 compute graph, two 81920-row kernels: 269 us avg
tp4 compute graph, four 40960-row kernels: 170 us avg
tp2 gather graph: 268 us avg
tp4 gather graph: 175 us avg
tp2 isolated graph with x copy: 322 us avg
tp4 isolated graph with x copies: 354 us avg
```

Interpretation:

- Geohot's expectation is correct for a truly huge single GEMV: `output.weight` row shard has real GPU graph speedup, about 2x for 2-way and about 3x for 4-way compute/gather in PROFILE.
- The wall-time loop sees less speedup because CPU/TinyJit dispatch overhead becomes visible in this microbench, but the GPU graph evidence is enough to prove the math/data movement can shard.
- This does not contradict the earlier full-decode result. The per-layer 60 MB MLA GEMV does not amortize multi-device graph overhead, and full Kimi decode has many small generated kernels plus layer-shard copies.
- The best next shard route is not generic per-layer GEMV TP. It is a correctness-safe `output.weight` TP argmax path, or a larger fused FFN/MLA region where communication stays inside the fused region. The previous output argmax TP had a TinyJit cross-device correctness issue, so that needs a minimal repro/fix before integration.

No production code change and no commit.

```text
4 78751 [78751, 78751]
```

Root cause found in the temp TP kernels: raw HIP `custom_kernel` UOps do not expose STORE/LOADs, so `ProgramInfo.from_sink` cannot infer `outs` and `ins`. Single-device execution often survives by queue order, but the cross-device path `GPU1 stage1 -> copy vals/idxs to GPU0 -> GPU0 stage2` can miss the dependency and read stale data.

Adding explicit `ProgramInfo(..., globals=..., outs=..., ins=...)` with 3D launch dims to the temp stage1/stage2 kernels fixed the real-weight TinyJit correctness:

```text
0 88768 [88768, 88768]
1 37324 [37324, 37324]
2 69067 [69067, 69067]
3 52669 [52669, 52669]
4 10619 [10619, 10619]
5 143626 [143626, 143626]
6 86729 [86729, 86729]
7 120423 [120423, 120423]
```

Then temporarily ported a production `kimi_output_argmax_q8_0_tp2` path behind `CUSTOM_KIMI_OUTPUT_ARGMAX_TP=1`, including `_ggml_raw_tp` attachment and `ProgramInfo` metadata. Direct correctness on real `output.weight` passed:

```text
0 66889 66889
1 99095 99095
2 121894 121894
3 1223 1223
4 30682 30682
5 75834 75834
6 18672 18672
7 112573 112573
```

But isolated production argmax speed only had a tiny signal:

```text
single median 393.154 us
tp2    median 385.002 us
```

Full Kimi with current best flags but `CUSTOM_KIMI_OUTPUT_ARGMAX_TP=1`:

```text
17.47 ms, 57.23 tok/s
17.25 ms, 57.96 tok/s
17.28 ms, 57.87 tok/s
17.27 ms, 57.91 tok/s
17.35 ms, 57.64 tok/s
```

Current-best regression after adding `ProgramInfo` metadata to the existing single output argmax/quant path:

```text
17.65 ms, 56.65 tok/s
17.42 ms, 57.41 tok/s
17.37 ms, 57.58 tok/s
17.28 ms, 57.88 tok/s
17.44 ms, 57.33 tok/s
```

Conclusion:

- The cross-device stale bug is real and the local fix is explicit custom-kernel `ProgramInfo`.
- For this path, correctness can be fixed, but speed does not improve the only target metric. The added dependency metadata and extra TP path slow the current `58.1-58.5 tok/s` recommendation.
- Reverted all production changes. Keep the temp repro scripts under `/tmp/b1` for future tinygrad custom-kernel dependency work.
- No commit.

## 2026-05-20 Forward Graph Cleanup Rejections

Audited `tinygrad/llm/model.py` for Kimi-hit graph cleanup targets after the TP routes. Kimi uses `MLATransformerBlock` because `kv_lora_rank=512`, has `61` layers, `leading_dense_block_count=1`, `expert_count=384`, `expert_used_count=8`, and `routed_scaling_factor=2.8269999027252197`.

Implications:

- The regular `TransformerBlock` attention mask TODO does not affect Kimi steady decode.
- The dense FFN `.contiguous()` TODO affects only block 0, so it is too small to be the next high-value target.
- The main common forward-level candidate is the block output `.contiguous()` in `FFNBlock.__call__`.

Tested removing the block output `.contiguous()`:

```diff
- return (h + self._feed_forward(self.ffn_norm(h))).contiguous()
+ return h + self._feed_forward(self.ffn_norm(h))
```

Command used current-best flags:

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 JITBEAM=8 REALIZE_SMALL=8000000 \
CUSTOM_KIMI_MOE_Q8=1 CUSTOM_KIMI_SHARED_Q8=1 CUSTOM_KIMI_ROUTER_TOPK=1 CUSTOM_KIMI_OUTPUT_ARGMAX_Q8=1 \
PYTHONPATH=. python3 tinygrad/llm/cli.py \
  --model /raid/gguf/kimi/Kimi-K2.6-UD-Q4_K_XL-00001-of-00014.gguf \
  --shard 3 --benchmark 8
```

Observed:

```text
17.31 ms, 57.78 tok/s
17.29 ms, 57.82 tok/s
17.18 ms, 58.21 tok/s
17.17 ms, 58.26 tok/s
17.29 ms, 57.83 tok/s
```

Conclusion: reject. This lands inside or below the existing `58.1-58.5 tok/s` range and does not repeat a speedup. The `.contiguous()` likely helps the existing graph/beam shape enough that removing it is neutral or slightly worse. Reverted, no commit.

Tested folding `routed_scaling_factor` into `kimi_router_topk` so the model can skip:

```py
probs = probs * self.config.routed_scaling_factor
```

The custom router kernel wrote `probs_out[tid] = probs_out[tid] * SCALE / norm` and the Kimi path passed `SCALE=2.8269999027252197`.

Full Kimi result:

```text
17.43 ms, 57.38 tok/s
17.24 ms, 58.00 tok/s
17.23 ms, 58.05 tok/s
17.22 ms, 58.08 tok/s
17.23 ms, 58.04 tok/s
```

Conclusion: reject. If the scalar multiply exists as a separate tiny op, removing it is below noise; in practice this is slower than the current repeated best. Reverted, no commit.

Current state after this audit: no production code diff. The next worthwhile speed work needs either a real profile-visible top-kernel change or a larger fused region; small forward cleanup is not producing measurable wins.

## 2026-05-20 Gate/Up Global XPack Probe

Tested whether selected gate/up can stop re-packing the same Q8 input in every CTA. Prototype:

```text
current:   kimi_quant_q8_0(x) -> kimi_gate_up_q4_q8_to_q8_0, each gate CTA packs xq into LDS
packed:    kimi_quant_q8_0(x) -> pack_xq once -> gate reads prepacked xps/xss
quantpack: quantize x and write q8+xps/xss in one kernel -> gate reads prepacked xps/xss
```

Temporary script:

```sh
/tmp/b1/bench_gateup_global_xpack.py
```

First two-way result before adding `quantpack`:

```text
diff 0
current      173.70 us
packed       154.50 us
```

PROFILE for that run showed the important caveat:

```text
current graph: kimi_quant_q8_0 12.82 us + kimi_gate_up_q4_q8_to_q8_0 39.14 us = batched graph 51.95 us
packed graph:  kimi_quant_q8_0 13.39 us + bench_pack_xq 8.79 us + gate 38.58 us = batched graph 60.77 us
```

So a separate pack kernel is not useful. Only `quantpack` could be viable, because quantization has the int8 q values in registers and can write `xps/xss` without rereading q8.

The first `quantpack` script revision faulted before producing numbers:

```text
RuntimeError: MMU fault: 0x0 | NotPresent=0 ReadOnly=0 NoExecute=0 imprecise=0
```

Cause: the temporary script put `bench_quant_pack_xq` and `bench_pack_xq` in the same HIP source, then launched the later symbol. This is the same known tinygrad AMD metadata/descriptor hazard from the packed-intermediate experiment. The script was fixed to split quant-pack and pack into separate compiled source strings.

Safe rerun after the source split:

```text
diff 0 0
current      149.25 us
packed       154.36 us
quantpack    150.01 us
```

PROFILE:

```text
current:   graph 52.04 us = kimi_quant_q8_0 13.54 us + gate/up 38.49 us
packed:    graph 60.34 us = kimi_quant_q8_0 13.51 us + pack_xq 8.65 us + gate/up 38.18 us
quantpack: graph 51.02 us = bench_quant_pack_xq 13.57 us + gate/up 37.44 us
```

Conclusion: reject. Fused quantpack saves only about `1 us/call`, roughly `0.06 ms/token` across 60 MoE layers, which is below trustworthy full-Kimi noise. No production code change and no commit.

## 2026-05-20 AITER-Style MoE/MLA Probe

Read local AITER/vLLM source again before testing:

- vLLM ROCm AITER MoE calls `aiter.fused_moe.fused_moe(...)` / asm MoE, not generic dequant matmul.
- AITER Kimi tuned MoE config has token=1 rows for `model_dim=7168`, `expert=384`, `topk=8`, and local `inter_dim=512/256`. That implies TP-sharded expert intermediate, not our current full `hidden_dim=2048` per GPU.
- AITER MoE decode uses tuned `block_m`, optional `ksplit`, non-temporal loads, and two-stage stage1/stage2 kernels. We already tested the direct Q4_0 versions of split-K and NT loads earlier; both were rejected.
- vLLM ROCm AITER MLA sparse backend supports `block_size=1` and calls `rocm_aiter_ops.mla_decode_fwd(...)`. The AITER MLA kernel returns `[tokens, heads, kv_lora_rank]`; the broader MLA module still handles projection modules around it.
- vLLM QuickReduce supports only world sizes 2/4/8 and has MB-scale minimum message sizes. Batch=1 row-parallel outputs like `7168 * fp16 ~= 14 KB` are below its threshold, so QuickReduce is not a direct answer for per-layer decode allreduce.

New proof-of-worth script:

```sh
/tmp/b1/bench_moe_hidden_tp.py
```

This tests AITER/vLLM-style MoE intermediate tensor parallelism:

- baseline: one GPU owns full selected MoE `hidden=2048`
- TP2: each GPU owns `hidden=1024`, computes topk=8 local gate/up and local down partial, then optionally reduces the 7168 output
- TP4: each GPU owns `hidden=512`
- weights are locally allocated and selected experts are spread across 384 expert ids: `[0, 54, 108, 162, 216, 270, 324, 383]`

Representative PROFILE results for `N=2 EXPERTS=384`:

```text
single_h2048 graph            ~74.9 us
  gate/up h2048               ~49.0 us
  down h2048                  ~28.6 us

local_h1024 graph             ~50-53 us
  gate/up h1024               ~29-30 us
  down h1024                  ~21-22 us

tp2_compute graph             ~65.0 us
tp2_reduce_one graph          ~62.1 us
tp2_allreduce graph           ~78.0 us
```

Representative PROFILE results for `N=4 EXPERTS=8`:

```text
single_h2048 graph            ~63.4 us
local_h512 graph              ~45-50 us
tp4_compute graph             ~69.2 us
tp4_reduce_one graph          ~99.3 us
```

Interpretation:

- Hidden-dim TP has real local compute reduction, unlike the earlier topk/expert-parallel route.
- The local reduction is not linear: halving hidden saves only about `20-25 us` on this Q4_0 kernel family, not 2x.
- Current tinygrad multi-GPU scheduling/reduce does not convert that local saving into a reliable end-to-end win. TP2 allreduce is tied/slower than single; TP4 is clearly slower.
- AITER's stronger result likely depends on its FP4/FP8 tensor-core fused MoE kernels and full TP stack, not just "split hidden and copy output" in tinygrad.

Conclusion: no production code and no commit. This is useful evidence, but not enough to start a Kimi TP rewrite in tinygrad unless we first get a faster local `h=512/256` MoE kernel or a fused/cheap allreduce path.

## 2026-05-20 Cross-GPU Producer Reduce Probe

Question: can we hide TP reduce by putting cross-GPU reduce into the producer path, especially MoE down or output argmax?

New script:

```sh
/tmp/b1/bench_moe_peer_reduce.py
```

The script first tests the smallest possible version: launch a custom kernel on `AMD` that directly reads an `AMD:1` buffer pointer and adds it to an `AMD` buffer. It then contains MoE variants for:

- current explicit copy reduce: `a + b.to(a.device)`
- direct peer add: producer on `AMD` reads `AMD:1`
- fused down producer: MoE down on `AMD` computes local partial and reads the remote partial before storing

Run:

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 NO_COLOR=1 JITBEAM=2 PYTHONPATH=. ZERO=1 EXPERTS=8 ITERS=8 REPS=2 \
python3 /tmp/b1/bench_moe_peer_reduce.py
```

Result:

```text
devs=('AMD', 'AMD:1') experts=8 hloc=1024 raw_gate=33.0MB sel=[0, 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7]
RuntimeError: MMU fault: 0x7ABD5B654000 | NotPresent=0 ReadOnly=0 NoExecute=0 imprecise=0
```

The fault happens in the initial `peer_add` smoke test, before the MoE down producer path.

Kernel log for the same address:

```text
[Wed May 20 14:08:03 2026] amdgpu 0000:05:00.0: amdgpu: [gfxhub0] retry page fault
address 0x00007abd5b654000
Faulty UTCL2 client ID: TCP (0x8)
PERMISSION_FAULTS: 0x3
MAPPING_ERROR: 0x0
RW: 0x0
```

This confirms the failing access is a shader/TCP read from the producer kernel, not an SDMA copy. `MAPPING_ERROR: 0x0` plus `PERMISSION_FAULTS: 0x3` points to peer access permission/mapping attributes, not a completely invalid virtual address.

Interpretation:

- A custom kernel on one AMD device cannot safely read a normal tinygrad buffer allocated on another AMD device by just receiving its pointer.
- This blocks direct producer-fused peer-read versions of MoE down and output argmax in the current runtime.
- To imitate vLLM QuickReduce-style behavior, tinygrad would first need runtime/allocator support for peer-visible scratch buffers or HIP IPC-style mapped buffers plus synchronization flags.
- Without that, the safe option remains explicit `.to()`/SDMA copy, and fusing the add into the producer can only save local add/launch overhead, not the cross-GPU transfer.

Conclusion: no production code and no commit. The direct cross-GPU producer-reduce path is not viable until there is a peer-mapped communication buffer smoke test that passes without MMU faults.

## 2026-05-20 Q8 GEMV TP Break-Even Sweep

Geohot discussion follow-up: quantify how large a batch=1 Q8_0 GEMV must be before row-shard TP wins.

New script:

```sh
/tmp/b1/bench_q8_tp_break_even.py
```

It uses real Kimi `output.weight` raw Q8_0 rows from the GGUF split and sweeps prefixes of the tensor. This keeps input dim fixed at `7168` and changes only output rows / raw weight size. For each size it measures:

- `single`: one GPU owns all rows
- `tp2_compute`: two GPUs compute half rows each, no gather
- `tp2_gather`: two GPUs compute half rows, copy/cat output to GPU0
- `tp4_compute`: four GPUs compute quarter rows each, no gather
- `tp4_gather`: four GPUs compute quarter rows, copy/cat output to GPU0

Command:

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 NO_COLOR=1 PYTHONPATH=. ITERS=64 REPS=3 \
python3 /tmp/b1/bench_q8_tp_break_even.py
```

Results:

```text
out    raw MB  single us  tp2 compute  tp2 gather  tp4 compute  tp4 gather  tp2x  tp4x
4096     29.8    155.996      164.721     174.802      311.117     339.483  0.89  0.46
8192     59.5    155.856      163.976     173.586      306.027     334.102  0.90  0.47
16384   119.0    155.386      163.031     176.819      308.432     333.175  0.88  0.47
32768   238.0    158.683      168.013     174.325      308.378     337.922  0.91  0.47
65536   476.0    221.041      170.569     175.793      311.363     340.458  1.26  0.65
98304   714.0    328.550      249.320     249.957      307.177     336.009  1.31  0.98
131072  952.0    411.384      294.470     297.836      311.420     339.456  1.38  1.21
163840 1190.0    537.668      336.344     339.944      309.129     338.615  1.58  1.59
```

VIZ/PROFILE subset:

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 NO_COLOR=1 PROFILE=1 VIZ=1 PYTHONPATH=. ITERS=8 REPS=1 OUTS=32768,65536,163840 \
python3 /tmp/b1/bench_q8_tp_break_even.py
```

Profile mode changes absolute wall times but preserves the trend:

```text
out=32768   238 MB: tp2x=0.92, tp4x=0.53
out=65536   476 MB: tp2x=1.01, tp4x=0.55
out=163840 1190 MB: tp2x=1.41, tp4x=1.16
```

VIZ artifacts:

```text
/tmp/b1/profile.pkl.b1tg
/tmp/b1/rewrites.pkl.b1tg
/tmp/b1/q8_tp_break_even_viz.json
```

Interpretation:

- TP2 break-even for this Q8_0 GEMV path is between about `238 MB` and `476 MB` raw weight size.
- TP4 break-even is much larger, around `1 GB` raw weight size.
- Kimi per-layer MLA/attention GEMVs around `60 MB` are far below the TP2 break-even point. This matches the earlier real `blk.1.attn_output.weight` result where compute-only TP was already slower.
- `output.weight` at about `1.19 GB` is large enough for TP. Geohot's intuition is right for this class of GEMV.
- Full Kimi decode does not have many repeated `1 GB` GEMVs. It has many dependent `30-120 MB` kernels plus MoE/custom kernels, so generic matrix TP is the wrong granularity for most of the decode path.

Conclusion: this gives a quantitative answer to the debate. "Batch=1 GEMV cannot shard" is false, but "every Kimi GEMV should shard" is also false. In this stack, Q8 GEMV TP needs several hundred MB of raw weight before 2-way starts to pay, and roughly 1GB before 4-way pays.

## 2026-05-20 Reduce Overhead Sweep

Question: is tinygrad reduce overhead large enough to erase tensor-parallel wins for batch=1 Kimi?

New script:

```sh
/tmp/b1/bench_reduce_sweep.py
/home/b1tg/back/tinygrad/kimi/bench_reduce_sweep.py
```

The script measures:

- `copy2_one`: `x0 + x1.to(AMD)`
- `copy2_bidir`: both `x0 + x1.to(AMD)` and `x0.to(AMD:1) + x1`
- `copy4_one`: copy three vectors to `AMD` and add
- `ar*_naive`: `UOp.allreduce(..., RING=0, ALL2ALL=0)`
- `ar*_ring`: forced `RING=2`
- `ar4_all2all`: forced `ALL2ALL=2`

Command:

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 NO_COLOR=1 PYTHONPATH=. ITERS=64 REPS=3 \
python3 /tmp/b1/bench_reduce_sweep.py
```

Results:

```text
n        MB/vector  copy2_one  copy2_bidir  ar2_naive  ar2_ring  copy4_one  ar4_naive  ar4_ring  ar4_all2all
7168        0.03      140.909      170.005    158.850   164.191    209.886    227.208   228.560      233.489
32768       0.12      154.444      171.749    169.588   168.191    214.245    238.166   236.352      236.956
262144      1.00      172.822      193.406    184.734   185.607    218.201    291.808   295.342      295.821
1048576     4.00      245.654      251.953    264.010   261.836    295.090    496.653   496.874      492.292
4194304    16.00      558.649      549.412    556.723   547.580    613.245   1328.929  1325.562     1324.445
```

`PROFILE=1 VIZ=1` subset:

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 NO_COLOR=1 PROFILE=1 VIZ=1 PYTHONPATH=. ITERS=8 REPS=1 SIZES=7168,1048576 \
python3 /tmp/b1/bench_reduce_sweep.py
```

Artifacts:

```text
/tmp/b1/profile_reduce_sweep.pkl
/tmp/b1/rewrites_reduce_sweep.pkl
/tmp/b1/reduce_sweep_viz.json
/home/b1tg/back/tinygrad/kimi/profile_reduce_sweep.pkl
/home/b1tg/back/tinygrad/kimi/rewrites_reduce_sweep.pkl
/home/b1tg/back/tinygrad/kimi/reduce_sweep_viz.json
```

Interpretation:

- `7168` fp32 elements is only about `28 KB`, but even the simplest 2-GPU copy+add path costs about `140 us` in this microbench. Bidirectional and `Ops.ALLREDUCE` forms are closer to `160-170 us`.
- For a Kimi MoE/TP hidden reduce, this is far too expensive to put after every layer. The target overhead would need to be single-digit to low tens of microseconds.
- Forced ring/all2all does not help for these small messages. This matches `tinygrad/schedule/allreduce.py`: small/2-node reductions default to naive because ring adds dispatch/chunking overhead.
- This explains why MoE hidden TP can show local compute savings but lose once the `7168` output is reduced.
- It does not explain the failed per-layer Q8 GEMV TP case, because that benchmark's compute-only path already lost before any gather/reduce.

Conclusion: yes, tinygrad's current small-message cross-GPU reduce path is too expensive for batch=1 Kimi TP. This looks like an implementation/runtime granularity problem, not a math problem. A useful TP path needs either no per-layer small reduce, a fused producer-side communication primitive, or a QuickReduce/IPC-style small-message path. Current `Ops.ALLREDUCE`/`.to()+add` is not enough.

## 2026-05-20 Peer-Mapped Reduce Runtime Probe

Question: can we bypass SDMA copy and make one GPU read a mapped peer buffer directly for the Kimi hidden-size reduce?

New script:

```sh
/tmp/b1/bench_peer_reduce_runtime.py
/home/b1tg/back/tinygrad/kimi/bench_peer_reduce_runtime.py
```

It allocates `a0/out0/scratch0` on `AMD`, `b1` on `AMD:1`, calls `AMD.allocator.map(b1._buf)`, then runs a tiny HIP add kernel on `AMD` reading either local `b0`, peer-mapped `b1`, or an SDMA-copied scratch buffer.

Stable small-message command:

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 NO_COLOR=1 PYTHONPATH=. MODE=single N=7168 ITERS=1024 REPS=5 \
python3 /tmp/b1/bench_peer_reduce_runtime.py
```

Results:

```text
devs AMD AMD:1 mode single n 7168 bytes 28672 iters 1024 reps 5
local_add          median=  64.609 us min=  64.277
peer_mapped_add    median=  64.845 us min=  64.521
sdma_only          median=  55.184 us min=  54.816
sdma_add           median= 127.336 us min= 127.046
```

Findings:

- Explicit peer mapping fixes the earlier direct peer-read MMU fault for this simple runtime kernel. `AMD` can read an `AMD:1` buffer after `d0.allocator.map(b1._buf)`.
- For a `7168` fp32 vector, the peer-mapped add is basically launch-bound: local and peer are both about `65 us`.
- This is about `2x` faster than SDMA-copy-then-add and about `2.4x` faster than the previous tinygrad `Ops.ALLREDUCE` 2-GPU result (`~159 us`), but it is still not low enough to make standalone per-layer TP hidden reduce clearly profitable.
- A separate `MODE=grid` showed that direct `d0.runtime(...)` HIP launch with `global_size=ceildiv(n, 256)` only wrote one CTA. `MODE=grid_threads` works for `N=7168`, but a large-N attempt timed out and is invalid. Do not use that failed large-N run as bandwidth evidence.

Conclusion: optimizing reduce is worth doing only as a fused/specialized path, not as a generic standalone tinygrad allreduce first. The useful target is to put the peer read/add into a producer or consumer kernel so the incremental cost is peer memory traffic, not a new `~65 us` launch per layer. Standalone small reduce would need to get below about `20 us` to change the MoE hidden-TP decision, and this proof does not show that yet.

## 2026-05-20 Fused Peer Reduce Consumer Probe

Question: can the hidden-vector reduce be hidden inside the next consumer kernel?

New script:

```sh
/tmp/b1/bench_peer_fused_consumer.py
/home/b1tg/back/tinygrad/kimi/bench_peer_fused_consumer.py
```

This tests a Kimi-shaped consumer: Q8_0 activation quantization of a `7168` fp32 hidden vector. It compares:

- `quant_local`: normal one-input Q8 quant
- `peer_add_quant`: peer-mapped add into a scratch vector, then Q8 quant
- `fused_peer_quant`: one Q8 quant kernel that reads `local[i] + peer[i]`

Command:

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 NO_COLOR=1 PYTHONPATH=. ITERS=1024 REPS=5 \
python3 /tmp/b1/bench_peer_fused_consumer.py
```

Results:

```text
quant_local          median=  63.939 us min=  63.789
local_add_quant      median= 128.728 us min= 128.587
peer_add_quant       median= 128.897 us min= 128.446
fused_local_quant    median=  64.627 us min=  64.221
fused_peer_quant     median=  64.422 us min=  64.130
```

Findings:

- Fusing `local + peer` into the consumer kernel removes the extra reduce launch. `peer_add_quant` is about `129 us`, while `fused_peer_quant` is about `64 us`.
- The peer read itself is not visible at this size: fused peer and fused local are both about one launch.
- This is the first reduce direction with a plausible win for batch=1 Kimi TP. It does not make standalone allreduce useful; it says any TP path must carry partial hidden states into the next consumer/producer and consume them with peer reads.
- The first draft faulted because the direct runtime dispatch shape could run extra workgroups for the quant kernel; adding a `block >= N/32` guard fixed correctness. Existing tinygrad custom kernels get their dispatch shape from `UOp.special`, so this guard is a microbench/runtime safety issue, not a Kimi math issue.

Conclusion: yes, peer read/add should be fused into producer/consumer kernels. The most promising concrete path is not `allreduce hidden -> next op`; it is `next op reads local hidden + mapped peer hidden` directly. For current Kimi custom paths, the natural first target is `kimi_quant_q8_0`: add a 2-way peer-sum variant and use it only in a TP experiment where both devices keep local partial hidden vectors.

## 2026-05-20 Exec Kernel Peer Automap Fix

Fixed the immediate integration blocker: ordinary `exec_kernel` did not map peer input buffers before launching a kernel. Graph runtime already maps inputs in `tinygrad/runtime/graph/hcq.py`, but non-graph kernel execution passed foreign-device pointers directly to the runtime.

Patch:

```text
tinygrad/engine/realize.py
```

Behavior added:

- For each kernel launch, if the running device allocator supports `map`, any global buffer whose `b.device != device` and whose backing object is an HCQ buffer is mapped into the launch device.
- This is intentionally narrow: it does not try to map CPU/DISK-like opaque buffers without HCQ ownership metadata.

Validation command, with no manual `Device["AMD"].allocator.map(...)` in the default path:

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 NO_COLOR=1 PYTHONPATH=. ITERS=128 REPS=3 \
python3 /tmp/b1/bench_kimi_quant_q8_sum_custom.py
```

Result:

```text
devs AMD AMD:1 dim 7168 iters 128 reps 3
tinyjit_quant_local    median= 113.774 us min= 113.261
tinyjit_quant_sum_peer median= 129.850 us min= 129.137
```

The script now also runs a non-JIT `kimi_quant_q8_sum_2way_0(x0, x1).realize()` before the TinyJit loop, so this directly verifies the ordinary `exec_kernel` automap path.

PROFILE artifact after automap:

```text
/home/b1tg/back/tinygrad/kimi/profile_kimi_quant_q8_sum_custom_automap.pkl
```

Kernel events remain in the same range:

```text
kimi_quant_q8_0              mostly 9-13 us
kimi_quant_q8_sum_2way_0     mostly 9-11 us
```

Conclusion: peer-sum consumer kernels can now be called without manual buffer mapping in the normal non-graph kernel path. This removes the mapping blocker for the next 2-way TP prototype.


Kernel-level profile:

```text
single_h2048:
  gateup_h2048 mostly ~39-41 us
  down_h2048   mostly ~25-28 us

tp2_fused_peerxq critical path:
  AMD:1 gateup_h1024 mostly ~29-32 us
  AMD:1 down_h1024   mostly ~22-23 us
  q8_sum_2way        mostly ~10-12 us
```

Interpretation:

- The fused consumer works and avoids the standalone reduce/add. `tp2_fused_quant` is faster than `tp2_reduce_one` and `tp2_allreduce`.
- Peer-reading input activation also works. `tp2_fused_peerxq` is the best TP variant in this bench.
- But the producer does not scale enough. Splitting `HIDDEN=2048 -> 1024+1024` only changes gateup from about `40 us` to about `30 us`, and down from about `25 us` to about `22 us`.
- After adding the fused q8 consumer, the TP critical path is roughly the same as the single-GPU kernel path, not materially faster. The isolated TinyJit wall time is worse because each bench call is a tiny graph with large launch overhead.

Conclusion: this specific MoE hidden-TP producer + fused Q8 consumer is not a clear path to Kimi speedup. The reduce problem is mostly fixed for this prototype; the remaining problem is that the gate/up and down producer kernels are not scaling enough when hidden is halved. Per the rule, this should be recorded, not committed as a speedup.

## 2026-05-20 Q4 Expert Preshuffle Microbench

Goal: test whether the current GGUF Q4_0 block layout itself is costing selected expert GEMV time. The current kernel reads each Q4 block as `[f16 scale][16 packed bytes]`. Two preshuffle layouts were tested:

- split layout: separate scale bytes and qdata bytes
- pair layout for gate/up: interleave gate and up scales/qdata per block, so one selected gate/up kernel reads adjacent gate/up payload

Bench script:

```text
/tmp/b1/bench_q4_preshuffle.py
/home/b1tg/back/tinygrad/kimi/bench_q4_preshuffle.py
```

Kimi-sized profile command:

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 NO_COLOR=1 PROFILE=1 PYTHONPATH=. EXPERTS=384 ITERS=8 ZERO=0 \
python3 /tmp/b1/bench_q4_preshuffle.py
```

Wall result:

```text
experts=384 gate_each=3.17GB down=3.17GB sel=[0,54,108,162,216,270,324,383]
gate_current 955.57 us
gate_split   1078.68 us
gate_pair     934.94 us
down_current 1493.34 us
down_split   1519.15 us
```

Profile artifact:

```text
/home/b1tg/back/tinygrad/kimi/profile_q4_preshuffle_pair_e384.pkl
```

Kernel-level result from `tinygrad.viz.cli`:

```text
gate current steady       mostly ~38.2-40.6 us
gate split steady         mostly ~36.1-38.3 us
gate pair steady          mostly ~36.2-37.5 us
down current steady       mostly ~26-28 us
down split steady         mostly ~25-27 us
```

Interpretation:

- Pair layout is a real but small selected gate/up kernel win: about `2-3 us/layer`.
- Split layout is a tiny selected down win: about `1 us/layer`.
- Across roughly 60 layers this is only about `0.2-0.3 ms/token`, while moving from about `59 tok/s` to `65 tok/s` needs about `1.4-1.7 ms/token`.
- A production version cannot keep both original and preshuffled expert weights in memory. It would need load-time replacement of `_ggml_raw` layout or loader support for directly producing the preshuffled buffers.

Conclusion: Q4 expert preshuffle has a measurable kernel-level signal, but it is not the next highest-value path to 65 tok/s by itself. Record it; do not commit production code until there is a larger combined layout/fusion win.

Interpretation:

- The fused kernel can remove a few microseconds versus two custom kernels, but only when the shared-down side is also the slower custom path.
- In real current-best Kimi, shared down plus selected add is already a generated fused `r_448...` bucket and is faster than this standalone custom shared-down path.
- Therefore this fusion is not enough to justify production work and cannot plausibly cover the `~1.5 ms/token` gap to 65 tok/s.

Conclusion: record only. This rejects the simple "fuse FFN tail" route as a primary 65 tok/s plan.

## 2026-05-20 Approx Top-N Expert Compute Probe

Goal: answer whether 65 tok/s is reachable by a route with certain speedup, even if it changes MoE math. Instead of changing router selection, keep the existing top-8 router result but only compute the first `N` selected experts in selected MoE gate/up and down.

Microbench script:

```text
/tmp/b1/bench_selected_topn.py
/home/b1tg/back/tinygrad/kimi/bench_selected_topn.py
```


```text

top4 gate/down: 27.19 us + 19.78 us
top2 gate/down: 23.47 us + 15.47 us
top1 gate/down: 23.10 us + 12.44 us
```

Interpretation:

- Reducing expert count does help, but not linearly. Gate/up hits a floor around `23 us` because the grid becomes too small to keep the GPU full.
- Top4 saves about `26 us/layer` in this isolated graph, enough to plausibly save about `1.5 ms/token` across 60 layers.
- Top5/top6 are unlikely to reach 65 tok/s; top4 is the likely threshold.

Temporary opt-in flag tested and then removed:

```text
tinygrad/llm/model.py
  uses the top-N selected expert kernels only when CUSTOM_KIMI_TOPK_N != num_experts_per_tok
```

## 2026-05-20 Exact 2GPU Selected-MoE EP Chain Recheck

Goal: revisit exact expert parallel after the peer-read mapping fixes. The rejected earlier EP proof used a tiny graph, where graph launch/sync overhead hid whether the two GPUs could overlap. This run simulates many layers inside one TinyJit graph to amortize graph overhead.

Bench script:

```text
/tmp/b1/bench_moe_ep_chain.py
/home/b1tg/back/tinygrad/kimi/bench_moe_ep_chain.py
```

It compares:

- `single_chain`: exact selected top8 gate/up + down on one GPU
- `ep_compute`: exact top4 + top4 on two GPUs, no output sum
- `ep_copy_sum`: exact top4 + top4, then `y0 + y1.to(AMD)`
- `ep_peer_sum`: exact top4 + top4, then one GPU peer-reads the other output in a custom add kernel

Command:

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 NO_COLOR=1 PYTHONPATH=/tmp/b1:. ZERO=0 LAYERS=60 ITERS=8 REPS=3 \
python3 /tmp/b1/bench_moe_ep_chain.py
```

Result:

```text
layers=60 iters=8 reps=3 zero=0
single_chain     median=   50.50 us/layer
ep_compute       median=   30.18 us/layer
ep_copy_sum      median=   38.90 us/layer
ep_peer_sum      median=   33.31 us/layer
```

Short PROFILE artifact:

```text
/home/b1tg/back/tinygrad/kimi/profile_moe_ep_chain_peer_sum_0520.pkl
```

Kernel timings in the chain profile:

```text
top8 gate/up  avg 35.80 us
top8 down     avg 25.92 us
top4 gate/up  avg 23.44 us
top4 down     avg 17.07 us
peer add      avg  9.47 us
```

Interpretation:

- This is exact MoE math: both top4 halves are computed and summed.
- Once graph overhead is amortized, two GPUs do overlap. The previous small-graph EP rejection was too pessimistic for a full-token graph.
- Peer-read sum is materially better than SDMA copy+add: `33.31 us/layer` vs `38.90 us/layer`.
- Net selected-MoE chain saving is about `17 us/layer`, or roughly `1.0 ms/token` over 60 layers. This is not enough alone to guarantee 65 tok/s, but it is now the largest exact speedup signal.

Production implication:

- This wants six GPUs: keep non-expert layer work on the existing 3 layer-shard GPUs, and place the other half of each layer's selected expert weights on three helper GPUs.
- The layer owner computes experts 0-3; its helper computes experts 4-7; the owner peer-reads and sums the helper selected output before the existing shared expert/add path.
- No expert is dropped, so quality is preserved.

Conclusion: exact selected-MoE EP with peer-read sum is worth pursuing. It is the first post-topN exact route with a `~1 ms/token` proof signal. It needs real weight placement across 6 GPUs and integration into the Kimi FFN path before we can know whether it reaches 65 tok/s.

Follow-up peer-xq chain result:

```text
layers=60 iters=8 reps=3 zero=0
single_chain     median=   50.62 us/layer
ep_compute       median=   30.24 us/layer
ep_copy_sum      median=   40.30 us/layer
ep_peer_sum      median=   33.52 us/layer
ep_peerxq_sum    median=   33.68 us/layer
```

This shows helper-side peer-reading of the 7.6KB `h_q8` activation is essentially free relative to local pre-copied `xq`.

Important caveat:

- The chain benchmark splits selected ranks `0..3` and `4..7`.
- For real Kimi this requires the helper GPU to have a full copy of that layer's selected expert weights, because any selected rank can point to any expert id.
- An expert-id partition would avoid duplication but needs dynamic per-device routing and has load-balance risk.

Temporary production prototype:

```text
CUSTOM_KIMI_MOE_EP=1
```

It duplicated each layer shard's selected expert raw weights to helper GPUs `AMD:3/4/5`, split selected ranks `0..3` and `4..7`, and used peer-read output sum. A small zero-weight smoke test passed with exact diff `0.0`.

Real Kimi command:

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 JITBEAM=8 REALIZE_SMALL=8000000 \
CUSTOM_KIMI_MOE_EP=1 CUSTOM_KIMI_ATTN_QAKV_HALF=1 CUSTOM_KIMI_MOE_Q8=1 CUSTOM_KIMI_SHARED_Q8=1 \
CUSTOM_KIMI_ROUTER_TOPK=1 CUSTOM_KIMI_OUTPUT_ARGMAX_Q8=1 \
PYTHONPATH=. python3 tinygrad/llm/cli.py \
  --model /raid/gguf/kimi/Kimi-K2.6-UD-Q4_K_XL-00001-of-00014.gguf \
  --shard 3 --benchmark 8
```

It did not produce a usable speed result. The run loaded as a `1.156 TB` effective model after duplicating selected experts, emitted two slow warmup tokens, then failed during rollout graph creation/execution with:

```text
RuntimeError: MMU fault: 0xFFFFFFBFE000 | NotPresent=0 ReadOnly=0 NoExecute=0 imprecise=0
HW fault: reset_type=0 reset_cause=0 memory_lost=1 gpu_id=53458
```

Conclusion update: keep the EP chain result as a strong exact proof signal, but reject the temporary production implementation. The `CUSTOM_KIMI_MOE_EP` code was removed. Next attempt should avoid full helper duplication or first isolate why the 6GPU duplicated-weight graph faults, preferably with a smaller multi-layer real-weight subset before trying full Kimi again.

User dmesg after the failed real 6GPU EP run:

```text
amdgpu 0000:85:00.0: [gfxhub0] retry page fault ...
in page starting at address 0x0000ffffffbfe000 from IH client 0x1b (UTCL2)
Faulty UTCL2 client ID: SQC (inst) (0x9)
PERMISSION_FAULTS: 0xb
MAPPING_ERROR: 0x0
RW: 0x0
Queue preemption failed ...
Queues reset on process python3 ...
amdgpu 0000:a6:00.0: ... Queue preemption failed ...
```

Interpretation: this is not the same class as the earlier TCP/SDMA data read faults. `SQC (inst)` means the failing client was instruction fetch, so this points more toward kernel code object / dispatch packet / program address / graph runtime state corruption than a normal peer-data load of `h_q8` or helper output. The bad address `0x0000ffffffbfe000` also looks like an invalid code/instruction VA. Therefore the next EP attempt should first isolate codegen/graph/runtime shape with a much smaller real-weight subset and the same device topology, not rerun full duplicated Kimi.

Reparsed the latest exact current-best profile without rerunning GPU:

```text
/home/b1tg/back/tinygrad/kimi/profile_kimi_exact_push65_0520.pkl
steady interval: decode @3..6
wall: 25.63 ms/token under PROFILE
```

Top repeated custom kernels per token on the three layer-shard GPUs:

```text
per GPU, 20 MoE layers:
kimi_gate_up_q4_q8_to_q8_0  ~1.06-1.07 ms/token, ~53 us/layer
kimi_down_reduce_q4_q8_0    ~0.71-0.72 ms/token, ~36 us/layer
kimi_shared_gate_up_q8_0    ~0.46-0.47 ms/token, ~23 us/layer
kimi_router_topk            ~0.40-0.41 ms/token, ~20 us/layer
kimi_quant_q8_0             ~0.19 ms/token on the owner-device bucket
output argmax stage1        ~0.35 ms/token on output owner
```

Interpretation:

- This confirms selected MoE is still the largest exact custom bucket, but it is not the whole token.
- The remaining generated MLA/attention tail is broad. Prior attempts already rejected fixed Q8 attention/output, MLA q custom, MLA v/softmax fusion, K/V layout, RMS, shared-down-add, and router variants.
- Exact selected-MoE EP remains the only measured path with about `~1 ms/token` signal, but the real full 6GPU prototype faulted in instruction fetch and must be isolated before another full run.
- Smaller exact wins still worth keeping queued: Q4 expert preshuffle (`~0.2-0.3 ms/token` expected) and output argmax TP (`~0.1-0.2 ms/token` scale), but neither alone reaches 65 tok/s.

Interpretation:

- Small graphs and the 6GPU custom/peer topology do not inherently fault.
- The 6GPU pairs timing is not directly comparable to current layer-shard decode, because the script runs three owner shards concurrently inside one graph.

Production integration attempt:

- Added experimental `CUSTOM_KIMI_MOE_EP=1`.
- `CUSTOM_KIMI_MOE_EP_LAYERS=N` limits how many MoE layers duplicate selected expert weights to helper GPUs.
- Initial unordered split computed `(k0..k3) + (k4..k7)`. It was fast but changed tokens, matching the earlier unacceptable top4-like behavior.
- Root cause was twofold:
  - Unordered split changes floating add grouping. Microbench diff was only around `1e-7`, but decode argmax can diverge.
  - Helper reading `h_q8` produced earlier in the same full graph did not preserve a reliable cross-device producer dependency; helper-side `h` copy + `kimi_quant_q8_0` restored the baseline token sequence for `EP_LAYERS=1`.

Exact ordered variant:

- Helper down now emits four per-expert vectors for k4..k7.
- Owner adds them back in the original top8 order.
- Microbench with rank-3 router-shaped `sel/probs` and high expert ids:

```text
FULL=1 SLICE=1 RANK3=1 HIGHSEL=1 ORDERED=1 LAYERS=60
diff 0.0
single 59.59 us/layer
ep     55.01 us/layer
```

Full Kimi, one EP layer, exact dependency path:

```text
CUSTOM_KIMI_MOE_EP=1 CUSTOM_KIMI_MOE_EP_LAYERS=1
steady output returned to baseline `empp` / `emppen` sequence
steady speed about 58.3-59.0 tok/s
```

Full Kimi, all 60 MoE layers, exact dependency path:

```text
CUSTOM_KIMI_MOE_EP=1 CUSTOM_KIMI_MOE_EP_LAYERS=60
using model "Kimi-K2.6" with 1,156,695,190,528 bytes and 1,598,092,588,544 params
44430.35 ms, 0.02 tok/s ...
8219.64 ms, 0.12 tok/s ...
RuntimeError: MMU fault: 0xFFFFFFBFE000 | NotPresent=0 ReadOnly=0 NoExecute=0 imprecise=0
HW fault: reset_type=0 reset_cause=0 memory_lost=1 gpu_id=53458
```

Conclusion:

- Exact selected-MoE EP is technically possible for a small graph and bitwise exact if helper outputs per-expert contributions and owner preserves add order.
- The exact version loses most of the original EP speed signal: `~4-5 us/layer` in microbench before charging helper-side `h` copy+quant.
- Full 60-layer Kimi still reproduces the same `0xFFFFFFBFE000` AMD fault as the earlier EP prototype.
- This route is not currently a commit candidate. It should remain recorded as an experiment unless the full-graph AMD fault can be eliminated and a real end-to-end speedup appears.

Cleanup after EP decision:

- Removed the no-speed selected-MoE EP production path from `tinygrad/llm/model.py`.
- Removed EP-only top4 gate/up, top4 down-reduce, per-expert down, and peer-add kernels from `tinygrad/llm/amd_kimi.py`.
- Removed unused peer-reduce/peer-map code from production diff; the remaining code diff is only the useful attention Q/KV-a fusion.
- Kept the reproducible bench scripts under `/home/b1tg/back/tinygrad/kimi/`.
- Kept the useful exact path changes unrelated to EP, especially `CUSTOM_KIMI_ATTN_QAKV_HALF`.

Next speed target:

- Do not continue EP first. The exact path has too little end-to-end signal and still triggers full-graph AMD faults at 60 layers.
- Recheck Q4 expert preshuffle before any production work. It is exact and local, but earlier notes already showed the post-bitpack win is small.
- After preshuffle recheck, only integrate if the kernel delta is large enough to plausibly move full decode; otherwise record and move on.

## 2026-05-20 Exact Selected-MoE EP Fault/Shape Recheck

Added a durable isolation script:

```text
/home/b1tg/back/tinygrad/kimi/bench_moe_ep_fault_isolate.py
```

It loads real Kimi Q4 expert raw weights directly from GGUF and can vary:

```text

```

First result without peer-buffer mapping:

```text
PAIRS=1 LAYERS=1 FULL=0 ORDERED=1
RuntimeError: MMU fault: 0x747BF2897000 | NotPresent=0 ReadOnly=0 NoExecute=0 imprecise=0
```

Interpretation: the custom peer-add kernel reading helper GPU output needs explicit peer-buffer mapping. This reproduces a basic data-read fault before reaching the earlier full-Kimi instruction-fetch fault.

Temporarily re-enabled peer mapping behind `HCQ_MAP_PEER_BUFS=1` for the experiment only. Minimal exact ordered compact then worked:

```text
PAIRS=1 LAYERS=1 FULL=0 ORDERED=1 HCQ_MAP_PEER_BUFS=1
diff 0.0
single_all 161.38 us/layer/pair
ep_all     720.05 us/layer/pair
```

Same-weight repeated-layer control using the older script still shows the optimistic EP signal:

```text
bench_moe_ep_real_layer.py
LAYERS=60 COMPACT=1 HCQ_MAP_PEER_BUFS=1
diff 2.384185791015625e-07
single_top8 58.35 us/layer
ep_peer     44.50 us/layer
```

But using distinct real layer weights removes the signal:

```text
PAIRS=1 LAYERS=20 FULL=0 ORDERED=0 HCQ_MAP_PEER_BUFS=1
diff 2.384185791015625e-07
single_all  98.12 us/layer/pair
ep_all     383.46 us/layer/pair

PAIRS=3 LAYERS=20 REAL_LAYER_MAP=1 FULL=0 ORDERED=0 HCQ_MAP_PEER_BUFS=1
diff 2.384185791015625e-07
single_all  85.89 us/layer/pair
ep_all     381.36 us/layer/pair
```

Full 384-expert raw for one layer does not fault with peer mapping, but is also slower:

```text
PAIRS=1 LAYERS=1 FULL=1 ORDERED=0 HCQ_MAP_PEER_BUFS=1
diff 2.384185791015625e-07
single_all 192.93 us/layer/pair
ep_all     293.82 us/layer/pair
```

Conclusion:

- The earlier same-weight repeated-layer EP microbench was optimistic and does not represent full Kimi's distinct-weight graph shape.
- Exact/realistic selected-MoE EP has no speed proof now. The realistic compact 6GPU shape is about `4.4x` slower per layer/pair.
- Peer mapping is required to avoid the basic data-read MMU fault, but enabling it does not produce a useful speed path here.
- The temporary `HCQ_MAP_PEER_BUFS` production changes were removed again per the no-speed/no-commit rule.
- Do not continue selected-MoE EP as the primary 65 tok/s route unless a new design avoids peer-read/map overhead and proves speed on distinct real layers first.

## 2026-05-20 AITER/vLLM Missing Pieces Recheck

Reread local AITER source after the vLLM pass:

```text
/home/b1tg/code/aiter/aiter/fused_moe.py
/home/b1tg/code/aiter/aiter/fused_moe_bf16_asm.py
/home/b1tg/code/aiter/aiter/mla.py
/home/b1tg/code/aiter/aiter/ops/fused_qk_rmsnorm_group_quant.py
/home/b1tg/code/aiter/op_tests/test_moe.py
/home/b1tg/code/aiter/op_tests/test_moe_2stage.py
/home/b1tg/code/aiter/aiter/configs/model_configs/kimik2_fp4_tuned_fmoe.csv
/home/b1tg/code/aiter/aiter/configs/model_configs/kimik2_fp8fp4_tuned_fmoe.csv
```

Important source-backed details:

- `fused_moe.py` calls a real sorted/two-stage MoE stack: `moe_sorting`, tuned metadata, optional FlyDSL kernels, CK tile fallbacks, `ksplit`, and non-temporal load selection. This is not equivalent to our selected GGUF Q4_0 GEMV kernels.
- Kimi tuned rows in `kimik2_fp4_tuned_fmoe.csv` include decode `token=1`, `model_dim=7168`, `expert=384`, `topk=8`, local `inter_dim=256/512`, with about `22-25 us` for FP4xFP4 two-stage MoE on the recorded target.
- `kimik2_fp8fp4_tuned_fmoe.csv` starts at larger token counts and uses `q_dtype_a=float8`, `q_dtype_w=float4`, again local `inter_dim=256/512`.
- AITER's `test_moe_2stage.py` explicitly benchmarks quant families including a16w4, a8w4, a4w4, a8w8, plus preshuffle and padding. The Kimi rows are packed FP4/FP8 layouts, not GGUF Q4_0 raw layout.
- `mla.py` uses an MLA decode kernel with stage1 plus a Triton stage2 reducer. That helps long-context decode attention; it does not directly remove the current short-benchmark MoE bottleneck.
- `fused_qk_rmsnorm_group_quant.py` fuses Q/K RMSNorm with FP8/FP4 group quant. We already tried the exact dual-RMS analogue and it did not improve full Kimi; the AITER win depends on feeding quantized attention kernels.

Decision:

- Do not port AITER MoE literally into current GGUF Q4_0 path. The missing win is a different representation and runtime stack: packed FP4/FP8 weights/activations, TP-sharded expert intermediate, tuned two-stage kernels, and a cheap TP reduce.
- The exact local analogues have already been tested and rejected or recorded: hidden TP `h=512/1024`, split-K, NT loads, preshuffle, dual RMS, MLA q/v fusions, shared down fusion, output TP, and selected-MoE EP.
- The only production diff that still has measured full-Kimi value is `CUSTOM_KIMI_ATTN_QAKV_HALF`. No new production code from this AITER pass.

Next exact speed routes with nonzero evidence:

- Keep current best flags and QAKV fusion.
- Revisit TP only if we first get either a faster local `h=512/256` Q4_0 selected-MoE kernel or a reliable peer-visible communication buffer with sub-20us fused reduce behavior.
- Otherwise the path to AITER-like speed requires a larger format conversion project: load/convert Kimi experts into an AITER-like packed FP4/FP8 layout and accept that this is no longer a simple GGUF Q4_0 exact-kernel optimization.

```text
bench_gateup_shape_t512      n=131 med=38.20 us avg=38.60 us
bench_gateup_shape_t256      n=131 med=37.36 us avg=37.80 us
bench_gateup_shape_t128      n=131 med=56.96 us avg=58.44 us
```

Interpretation:

- The wall-time signal was misleading under PROFILE overhead. Real AMD kernel timestamps say current 1024-thread/32-warp gate/up is still best or tied.
- Reducing to 512/256 threads serializes multiple rows per warp and loses more than it saves. 128 threads is clearly bad.
- The 32-row CTA is not arbitrary: it matches one Q8_0 output block and computes one scale over exactly 32 hidden values. Smaller row CTAs would change the quantization block unless two CTAs cooperated, which is not a simple retune.
- The inner `j += 4` sdot4 loop is already the natural packed unit for Q4/Q8. Unrolling it is the right choice.

Router thread sweep recheck:

```text
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 NO_COLOR=1 PROFILE=1 JITBEAM=2 PYTHONPATH=. ITERS=4096 \
python3 /home/b1tg/back/tinygrad/kimi/bench_router_topk_sweep.py
```

Wall result:

```text
generic        121.57 us
current         98.61 us
t512            98.65 us
t256           116.60 us
t128            98.61 us
t64             99.11 us
```

The variants are effectively tied at graph wall time; PROFILE saw only graph-level kernels because TinyJit captured the hot loop. No production change.

Decision:

- Do not change gate/up thread count or router thread count.
- The obvious `THREADS`, `ROWS`, `WARPS_PER_ROW`, and `#pragma unroll` retunes have now mostly been swept. Remaining single-kernel retuning is unlikely to bridge the 59 -> 65 tok/s gap.
- Future exact work should target larger structural changes with proof signal, not cosmetic constants: packed expert layout/preshuffle combined with loader support, or a new exact MoE/MLA representation that materially reduces bytes read per token.

## 2026-05-20 Selected Down Deferred Exact Reduce

Tested whether `kimi_down_reduce_q4_q8_0` spends avoidable time synchronizing across the two warps per row. The old kernel did, for every topk expert:

```text
warp partial write -> syncthreads -> warp0 reduce partials -> syncthreads
```

Added durable microbench:

```text
/home/b1tg/back/tinygrad/kimi/bench_down_late_reduce.py
```

It tests two variants:

- `late_reduce`: each warp accumulates `sum * prob` locally, then cross-warp reduces once. This is fastest-ish but changes floating add grouping.
- `defer_exact`: write all `(topk, row, warp)` partial sums to shared memory, synchronize once, then reduce in the original `for k -> for warp` order. This preserves the current final accumulation order.

Command:

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 NO_COLOR=1 PROFILE=1 JITBEAM=2 PYTHONPATH=. ITERS=256 EXPERTS=384 \
python3 /home/b1tg/back/tinygrad/kimi/bench_down_late_reduce.py
```

Before production patch, PROFILE medians:

```text
bench_down_deferred_exact      26.52 us
bench_down_late_reduce         26.34 us
kimi_down_reduce_q4_q8_0       27.12 us
```

Integrated the `defer_exact` shape into `tinygrad/llm/amd_kimi.py`: `partial` is now `TOPK * ROWS * WARPS_PER_ROW`, the per-topk inner synchronizations are removed, and final reduction happens once after all topk partials are ready.

After production patch, short microbench:

```text
kimi_down_reduce_q4_q8_0       26.40 us
bench_down_deferred_exact      26.34 us
bench_down_late_reduce         26.28 us
```

Full Kimi validation:

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 JITBEAM=8 REALIZE_SMALL=8000000 \
CUSTOM_KIMI_MOE_Q8=1 CUSTOM_KIMI_SHARED_Q8=1 CUSTOM_KIMI_ROUTER_TOPK=1 CUSTOM_KIMI_OUTPUT_ARGMAX_Q8=1 CUSTOM_KIMI_ATTN_QAKV_HALF=1 \
PYTHONPATH=. python3 tinygrad/llm/cli.py \
  --model /raid/gguf/kimi/Kimi-K2.6-UD-Q4_K_XL-00001-of-00014.gguf \
  --shard 3 --benchmark 12
```

Observed steady decode:

```text
16.90 ms, 59.16 tok/s
16.75 ms, 59.69 tok/s
16.73 ms, 59.78 tok/s
16.82 ms, 59.45 tok/s
16.81 ms, 59.48 tok/s
16.87 ms, 59.28 tok/s
16.87 ms, 59.29 tok/s
16.83 ms, 59.43 tok/s
16.86 ms, 59.33 tok/s
```

Output stayed on the known `emppen...` trajectory.

Validation:

```text
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 python3 -m ruff check tinygrad/llm/amd_kimi.py
All checks passed.
```

Decision: keep this production change. The gain is small, roughly `0.6-0.8 us/layer` in the selected down kernel, but it is exact-order, local, and repeats in full Kimi with a new best around `59.7 tok/s`. It does not close the 65 tok/s gap by itself.

Current-best PROFILE refresh after the down change:

```sh

CUSTOM_KIMI_MOE_Q8=1 CUSTOM_KIMI_SHARED_Q8=1 CUSTOM_KIMI_ROUTER_TOPK=1 CUSTOM_KIMI_OUTPUT_ARGMAX_Q8=1 CUSTOM_KIMI_ATTN_QAKV_HALF=1 \
PYTHONPATH=. python3 tinygrad/llm/cli.py \
  --model /raid/gguf/kimi/Kimi-K2.6-UD-Q4_K_XL-00001-of-00014.gguf \

```

Named `amd_kimi.py` buckets in the captured decode graph:

```text
kimi_output_argmax_q8_stage1  ~407.6 us/token
```

Interpretation: selected gate/up remains the largest named custom bucket, but its obvious local variants have already been tested. Output argmax is a single-token bucket worth checking when a shape idea is cheap.

Question: after the visible constant/unroll sweeps, do the compiled HIP kernels show resource or wavefront issues worth targeting?

Added durable codegen audit script:

```text
/home/b1tg/back/tinygrad/kimi/dump_amd_kimi_codegen.py
/home/b1tg/back/tinygrad/kimi/amd_kimi_codegen_current_0520.csv
```

Command:

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 PYTHONPATH=. python3 /home/b1tg/back/tinygrad/kimi/dump_amd_kimi_codegen.py \
  | tee /home/b1tg/back/tinygrad/kimi/amd_kimi_codegen_current_0520.csv
```

Important rows:

```text
kimi_gate_up_q4_q8_to_q8_0   wg=1024 lds=8192  vgpr=55 spill=0 wave=64 inst=636  v_dot=16
kimi_down_reduce_q4_q8_0     wg=512  lds=18944 vgpr=43 spill=0 wave=64 inst=1227 v_dot=64
kimi_shared_gate_up_q8_0     wg=32   lds=0     vgpr=63 spill=0 wave=64 inst=518  v_dot=16
kimi_router_topk             wg=32   lds=32    vgpr=58 spill=0 wave=64 inst=3460 v_exp=12 v_div=52
kimi_quant_q8_0              wg=32   lds=0     vgpr=8  spill=0 wave=64 inst=373
```

Observations:

- No hot production kernel is spilling VGPR/SGPR, so there is no obvious occupancy cliff from current unroll choices.
- `__launch_bounds__(THREADS, N)` for gate/down/output does not change VGPR, SGPR, LDS, or instruction count on gfx942.
- `-mno-wavefrontsize64` compiles but still emits `.wavefront_size: 64` for gfx942, so a compiler flag cannot make the 32-thread kernels wave32 here.
- The only new suspicious kernel was router: it keeps `p[12]`, `s[12]`, and `idx[12]`, giving high VGPR and a large unrolled body.

Tested a router variant that keeps only scores and recomputes sigmoid probability for the 8 selected winners. This drops VGPR but adds winner `exp2/div` work:

```text
current   vgpr=58 sgpr=18 lds=32 wave=64 inst=3460 exp=12 div=52
recompute vgpr=42 sgpr=20 lds=32 wave=64 inst=3500 exp=20 div=84
```

Bench command:

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 NO_COLOR=1 PROFILE=1 JITBEAM=2 PYTHONPATH=. NOJIT=1 ITERS=512 \
python3 /home/b1tg/back/tinygrad/kimi/bench_router_warp32.py
```

Correctness matched (`diff 0.0`). Repeated PROFILE medians:

```text
kimi_router_topk                 19.440 us
bench_router_warp32              19.400 us
bench_router_lane0_local         19.560 us
bench_router_lane0_global        19.760 us
bench_router_recompute_prob      20.520 us
bench_router_localtop            25.320 us
```

Decision: reject `recompute_prob`. The register reduction is real, but the extra sigmoid work makes the kernel about `1.1 us/layer` slower. No production change.

Side note: using 64 active lanes for the 32-thread GEMV kernels is not an exact local tweak. For shared gate/up or one-row GEMV, splitting the 224 Q8 blocks across 64 lanes changes the FP accumulation order versus the current one-warp order. With the no-quality-compromise rule, do not pursue that as a production path unless it is explicitly treated as an accuracy-changing experiment.

Validation:

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 python3 -m ruff check /home/b1tg/back/tinygrad/kimi/bench_router_warp32.py /home/b1tg/back/tinygrad/kimi/dump_amd_kimi_codegen.py
```

## 2026-05-20 Down Q4 Sign Add/Xor Kept

Question: can the hot Q4 nibble sign-extension in `kimi_down_reduce_q4_q8_0` be cheaper?

The existing helper expands four signed Q4 nibbles with shift/or sign replication:

```cpp
uint32_t s = r & 0x08080808u;
return int(r | (s << 1) | (s << 2) | (s << 3) | (s << 4));
```

Tested a byte-independent add/xor mapping:

```cpp
((nibble + 0x78) ^ 0x80)
```

For `nibble in 0..15`, this exactly produces `(nibble - 8) & 255` without cross-byte carry because the byte range is `0x78..0x87`.

Durable bench:

```text
/home/b1tg/back/tinygrad/kimi/bench_q4sign_variant.py
```

Pre-production PROFILE, `EXPERTS=384 ITERS=256`:

```text
kimi_gate_up_q4_q8_to_q8_0      med 39.200 us avg 40.110 us
bench_gateup_q4sign_mul         med 39.160 us avg 39.459 us
bench_gateup_q4sign_addxor      med 39.280 us avg 39.636 us

kimi_down_reduce_q4_q8_0        med 25.840 us avg 26.365 us
bench_down_q4sign_mul           med 25.840 us avg 26.349 us
bench_down_q4sign_addxor        med 25.160 us avg 25.665 us
```

Decision: keep only the down change. Gate/up does not get a reliable win, but down repeats around `0.7 us/layer` faster. Expected full-decode upside is small, roughly `0.04 ms/token`, but it is exact and local.

Full Kimi sanity after production down patch:

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 JITBEAM=8 REALIZE_SMALL=8000000 \
CUSTOM_KIMI_MOE_Q8=1 CUSTOM_KIMI_SHARED_Q8=1 CUSTOM_KIMI_ROUTER_TOPK=1 CUSTOM_KIMI_OUTPUT_ARGMAX_Q8=1 CUSTOM_KIMI_ATTN_QAKV_HALF=1 \
PYTHONPATH=. python3 tinygrad/llm/cli.py \
  --model /raid/gguf/kimi/Kimi-K2.6-UD-Q4_K_XL-00001-of-00014.gguf \
  --shard 3 --benchmark 8
```

```text
16.81 ms, 59.48 tok/s
16.78 ms, 59.61 tok/s
16.62 ms, 60.18 tok/s
16.67 ms, 60.00 tok/s
16.66 ms, 60.03 tok/s
```

This is not enough for 65 tok/s by itself, but it preserves the current-best speed band and gives a small exact kernel win.

Validation:

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 python3 -m ruff check tinygrad/llm/amd_kimi.py /home/b1tg/back/tinygrad/kimi/bench_q4sign_variant.py
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 python3 -m unittest test.unit.test_llm_moe
```

## 2026-05-20 Gate/Up Hidden-Block Grouping Rejected

Question: can selected gate/up reduce repeated Q8 activation staging by grouping multiple 32-row hidden blocks into one CTA? Current production launches one CTA per `(topk, hidden_block)` and each CTA stages the same 7.6KB Q8 activation into LDS. Grouping `2/4/8` hidden blocks per CTA halves or better the CTA count and LDS staging, while keeping the same per-row dot and quantization order.

Added durable bench:

```text
/home/b1tg/back/tinygrad/kimi/bench_gateup_hblock_group.py
```

First version quantized after each hidden block inside the grouped CTA. It had misleading wall-time but slower PROFILE:

```text
current                      med 39.760 us
bench_gateup_hgrp2           med 41.280 us
bench_gateup_hgrp4           med 63.700 us
bench_gateup_hgrp8           med 112.560 us
```

Then changed the prototype to defer quantization: compute all grouped hidden blocks into `vals[HGRP * 32]`, synchronize once, then warp0 quantizes all grouped blocks. This removes the per-hidden-block sync/quant barrier from the first grouped attempt.

Command:

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 PROFILE=1 NO_COLOR=1 JITBEAM=2 PYTHONPATH=. EXPERTS=384 ITERS=128 \
python3 /home/b1tg/back/tinygrad/kimi/bench_gateup_hblock_group.py
```

Deferred PROFILE:

```text
kimi_gate_up_q4_q8_to_q8_0       med 39.680 us avg 40.074 us
bench_gateup_hgrp2_defer         med 40.640 us avg 42.088 us
bench_gateup_hgrp4_defer         med 58.600 us avg 59.079 us
bench_gateup_hgrp8_defer         med 98.480 us avg 98.869 us
```

Decision: reject. The reduced staging/CTA count does not beat current one-hidden-block-per-CTA parallelism. `hgrp2` is close but still slower at the actual GPU timestamp level; `hgrp4/8` lose heavily. No production code change.

Validation:

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 python3 -m ruff check /home/b1tg/back/tinygrad/kimi/bench_gateup_hblock_group.py
```

## 2026-05-20 Output Argmax Atomic Rejected

Question: can `kimi_output_argmax_q8_0` avoid writing `5120` intermediate `(val, idx)` pairs and the stage2 reduce by doing a global `atomicMax` in stage1?

Prototype:

```text
current: stage1 writes vals/idxs -> stage2 reduces 5120 groups
atomic:  init uint64 key -> stage1 computes each group best and atomicMax(key) -> final decodes idx
```

The atomic key stores ordered-float high bits plus `0xffffffff - idx` low bits, preserving the existing lower-index tie break for finite values.

Added durable bench:

```text
/home/b1tg/back/tinygrad/kimi/bench_output_argmax_atomic.py
```

Command:

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 PROFILE=1 NO_COLOR=1 JITBEAM=2 PYTHONPATH=. ITERS=64 \
python3 /home/b1tg/back/tinygrad/kimi/bench_output_argmax_atomic.py
```

Correctness on zero Q8 input/weights:

```text

```

PROFILE:

```text
kimi_output_argmax_q8_0_stage1      med 309.960 us
kimi_output_argmax_q8_0_stage2      med  12.440 us

bench_output_atomic_init            med   8.360 us
bench_output_argmax_atomic_stage    med 310.120 us
bench_output_atomic_final           med   8.720 us
```

Decision: reject. Atomic stage1 is essentially tied with current stage1, but `init + final` costs about `17 us`, more than the current stage2 reduce at about `12 us`. No production code change.

Validation:

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 python3 -m ruff check /home/b1tg/back/tinygrad/kimi/bench_output_argmax_atomic.py
```

## 2026-05-20 Router Local-Top8 Rejected

Question: can `kimi_router_topk` avoid rescanning each lane's 12 local experts for every top-k selection? Current warp32 router does `8` rounds; each round scans `PER_LANE=12`, does a warp reduce, then masks the winning local score. A possible exact alternative is:

```text
per lane: compute sorted local top8 from 12 scores
per k:    reduce only the current local candidate across lanes
winner lane advances its local pointer
```

This is exact for global top8 because a lane's 9th local candidate cannot enter the global top8 unless at least 8 same-lane candidates already did.

Updated durable bench:

```text
/home/b1tg/back/tinygrad/kimi/bench_router_warp32.py
```

Added `bench_router_localtop` and a `NOJIT=1` mode to collect repeated per-dispatch kernel timestamps.

Command:

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 PROFILE=1 NO_COLOR=1 JITBEAM=2 PYTHONPATH=. NOJIT=1 ITERS=2048 \
python3 /home/b1tg/back/tinygrad/kimi/bench_router_warp32.py
```

Correctness matched current output on random logits/bias:

```text
localtop sel matched, diff 0.0
```

NOJIT PROFILE:

```text
kimi_router_topk             med 20.080 us avg 20.347 us
bench_router_warp32          med 20.080 us avg 20.357 us
bench_router_lane0_local     med 20.080 us avg 20.378 us
bench_router_lane0_global    med 20.360 us avg 20.636 us
bench_router_localtop        med 25.760 us avg 26.015 us
```

Decision: reject. The local top8 pre-sort increases register pressure and work enough that it is much slower than simply rescanning 12 local scores per top-k round. Keep production `kimi_router_topk`.

Validation:

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 python3 -m ruff check /home/b1tg/back/tinygrad/kimi/bench_router_warp32.py
```

## 2026-05-20 Q8 Quant Store Packing Rejected

Question: can `kimi_quant_q8_0` reduce payload store overhead by having fewer lanes write multiple quantized bytes? Prior quant sweeps covered CTA grouping and rounding, but not payload store packing. `kimi_quant_q8_0` appears about 61 times per token in current-best profile, so even small per-call savings are worth measuring.

Added durable bench:

```text
/home/b1tg/back/tinygrad/kimi/bench_quant_q8_storepack.py
```

First tried unaligned 32-bit/64-bit stores into the Q8 payload at `base + 2`. Those were not correct (`diff 255`), so the valid variants use all-lane `__shfl` followed by grouped byte stores:

```text
byte4: lanes 0..7 each write 4 bytes
byte8: lanes 0..3 each write 8 bytes
```

Command:

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 PROFILE=1 NO_COLOR=1 JITBEAM=2 PYTHONPATH=. ITERS=4096 \
python3 /home/b1tg/back/tinygrad/kimi/bench_quant_q8_storepack.py
```

Correctness:

```text

byte8 diff 0
```

PROFILE:

```text
kimi_quant_q8_0       med 11.000 us avg 11.220 us
bench_quant_q8_byte4  med 10.920 us avg 11.163 us
bench_quant_q8_byte8  med 11.000 us avg 11.229 us
```

Decision: reject. `byte4` is only about `0.08 us/call` faster at median, roughly `0.005 ms/token` across 61 calls, and the more aggressive wide stores are incorrect for this unaligned payload layout. Keep current simple per-lane byte store.

Validation:


## 2026-05-20 amd_kimi Visible Knobs Rerun

Question: are the visible loop constants, launch bounds, and unroll pragmas in `tinygrad/llm/amd_kimi.py` already locally optimal enough, or is there still a safe production squeeze?

Fixed two measurement issues in the durable knob script:

- `/home/b1tg/back/tinygrad/kimi/bench_amd_kimi_knobs.py` now realizes `list` outputs from `Tensor.custom_kernel(... )[:2]`.
- `SECTION=output` now compares actual production stage1 launch bounds, not a mislabeled `lb1` variant.

Commands:

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 PROFILE=1 NO_COLOR=1 JITBEAM=2 PYTHONPATH=. SECTION=gate ITERS=256 EXPERTS=384 \
python3 /home/b1tg/back/tinygrad/kimi/bench_amd_kimi_knobs.py
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 PROFILE=1 NO_COLOR=1 JITBEAM=2 PYTHONPATH=. SECTION=down ITERS=256 EXPERTS=384 \
python3 /home/b1tg/back/tinygrad/kimi/bench_amd_kimi_knobs.py
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 PROFILE=1 NO_COLOR=1 JITBEAM=2 PYTHONPATH=. SECTION=output ITERS=256 EXPERTS=384 \
python3 /home/b1tg/back/tinygrad/kimi/bench_amd_kimi_knobs.py
```

PROFILE medians:

```text
gate current                 39.040 us
gate lb1                     39.040 us
gate lb2                     38.920 us avg worse
gate nounroll_inner          54.000 us

down current                 27.040 us
down wpr1_exact_r8           26.040 us
down wpr1_exact_r16          27.760 us

output stage1 current       300.800 us
output stage1 lb1           300.800 us
output stage1 lb2           300.880 us
output stage1 nounroll      576.400 us
```

Interpretation:

- Gate/up: current `THREADS=1024`, one warp per output row, and inner unroll are still the right production choices. Launch-bound variants are noise, and removing inner unroll is clearly bad.
- Down: WPR1 still has the old isolated `~1 us/call` temptation, but this was already rejected in full Kimi because it changes FP reduction behavior and benchmark token trajectory. With no quality compromise, keep the current WPR2 deferred exact reduce.
- Output argmax stage1: production, `lb1`, and `lb2` are tied within measurement noise; inner nounroll is much worse.

Decision: no production change. The visible constants/unroll knobs in `amd_kimi.py` are effectively exhausted for exact decode. Remaining local deltas are at most `~0.04-0.06 ms/token`, below trustworthy full-decode noise, or require changing numeric order.

Validation:

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 python3 -m ruff check /home/b1tg/back/tinygrad/kimi/bench_amd_kimi_knobs.py
```

## 2026-05-20 Expert-ID Partition EP Probe

Question: can selected-MoE expert parallel avoid full helper duplication by putting expert ids `0..191` on GPU0 and `192..383` on GPU1, while both GPUs receive the same top8 router output?

Added durable script:

```text
/home/b1tg/back/tinygrad/kimi/bench_moe_ep_id_partition.py
```

The script has:

- `MODE=local`: run partition gate/down on both GPUs and realize both outputs, but do not reduce them.
- `MODE=reduce`: add the two partial outputs with `peer_add`; this currently faults on this path.
- `MODE=ordered` / `MODE=ordered_copy`: experimental exact-order per-topk contribution reduction paths, not used for the decision because local compute already lost.

First, synthetic sanity:

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 NO_COLOR=1 JITBEAM=2 PYTHONPATH=. REAL=0 MODE=single LAYERS=1 ITERS=1 REPS=1 \
python3 /home/b1tg/back/tinygrad/kimi/bench_moe_ep_id_partition.py
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 NO_COLOR=1 JITBEAM=2 PYTHONPATH=. REAL=0 MODE=local LAYERS=1 ITERS=2 REPS=1 \
python3 /home/b1tg/back/tinygrad/kimi/bench_moe_ep_id_partition.py
```

Output:

```text
single   234.86 us/layer
ep_local 300.65 us/layer
```

Then real Kimi layer 1, balanced selected ids `[0, 54, 108, 162, 216, 270, 324, 383]`:

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 NO_COLOR=1 JITBEAM=2 PYTHONPATH=. REAL=1 MODE=local LAYER=1 LAYERS=1 ITERS=2 REPS=2 \
```

```text
single   median=197.71 us/layer min=192.06 vals=[203.36, 192.06]
ep_local median=300.03 us/layer min=290.12 vals=[309.94, 290.12]
```

A repeat with `PROFILE=1` had the same wall direction:

```text
single   234.97 us/layer
ep_local 358.22 us/layer
```

`MODE=reduce` with and without `HCQ_MAP_PEER_BUFS=1` hit AMD MMU faults at the synchronization after `peer_add(y0, y1)`. Since `MODE=local` succeeds, the partition gate/down kernels are runnable; the fault is in the cross-GPU partial reduce path or its mapping. But this does not block the decision: even with the reduce removed entirely, id-partition EP is already slower than single GPU for the real layer.

Decision: reject this expert-id partition design as a speed path. It avoids full expert duplication, but the current tinygrad scheduling/custom-kernel shape does not produce useful overlap; adding any exact or inexact cross-GPU reduction can only make it worse. No production change.

## 2026-05-20 Q4 Sign-Extend Variant Rejected

Question: can the Q4 signed nibble unpack in selected gate/up and selected down be cheaper?

Current helper:

```c
uint32_t s = r & 0x08080808u;
return int(r | (s << 1) | (s << 2) | (s << 3) | (s << 4));
```

Tested an exact multiply form:

```c
return int(r | ((r & 0x08080808u) * 30u));
```

Command:

```sh
TMPDIR=/tmp/b1 TMP=/tmp/b1 TEMP=/tmp/b1 NO_COLOR=1 PROFILE=1 JITBEAM=2 PYTHONPATH=. ITERS=128 \
python3 /home/b1tg/back/tinygrad/kimi/bench_q4sign_variant.py
```

Wall output looked mixed and misleading:

```text

gate_mul       911.72 us
down_current  1488.34 us
down_mul      1586.39 us
```

PROFILE kernel timestamps rejected the multiply form:

```text

bench_gateup_q4sign_mul      median 39.88 us avg 40.50 us
kimi_down_reduce_q4_q8_0     median 27.08 us avg 27.61 us
bench_down_q4sign_mul        median 27.24 us avg 28.02 us
```

Decision: keep the current shift/or `q4sign`. The multiply form is exact but not faster in the real AMD kernel timestamp.
